A backend mismatch follows the ordinary cache-mismatch policy: disk
fallback when enabled, an error otherwise. New engines can connect to
existing CUDA IPC daemons; VMM daemons reject engines that do not
advertise VMM support.

After loading, post-processing and warmup, the daemon calls
`build_manifest` to record the final canonical parameters and buffers as
logical views over distinct storages, including any tensors created or
replaced during warmup. It then materializes the storages, drops the
model and releases startup runtime resources. VMM moves non-empty GPU
storages into exportable `cuMemCreate` or `hipMemCreate` allocations. By
default, consecutive storages are packed in input order toward a 1 GiB
logical cap, with 512-byte alignment between logical storages. An
oversized storage gets its own allocation, and every allocation is
rounded up to the driver's granularity. Set
`--weight-cache-vmm-max-allocation-bytes` to change the cap, or to `0`
to use one allocation per storage. Each pack is copied and synchronized
separately, avoiding retention of a second complete model. VMM handles
only allocation and FD export/import; the manifest preserves the logical
layout, including tied parameters, shared storages, offsets and strides.
Each `StorageRecord` holds `owner`, `storage` and `byte_offset`. The
manifest records each logical storage's `nbytes`, while
`record.storage.nbytes()` reports the physical size of its potentially
shared allocation. Granularity padding counts once per unique owner in
the external-weight memory budget. CPU and empty storages continue to
use the existing by-value transfer path.

|vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.protocol.WeightCacheState|
carries `manifest`, `storages`, `aliases` and `attrs` on both sides; the
producer and consumer differ only in where they use the same state type.
The daemon captures its state before starting listeners or reporting
readiness. For each `get_state` consumer, the daemon's backend exports
every storage in the state once. CUDA IPC still creates fresh
per-consumer handle reductions. VMM entries hold
|vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.ipc_backends.serial_utils.SharedFileDescriptor|
objects: the existing pickle persistent-ID protocol encodes their
references, while `SCM_RIGHTS` passes the actual FDs over the
owner-verified Unix socket, never as serialized process-local integers.
The consumer calls `rebuild_entries` to obtain `StorageRecord`s,
deduplicating imports for entries that reference the same descriptor so
each packed allocation is imported only once. It then constructs
|vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.protocol.WeightCacheState|
and rebuilds all tensor views from the manifest. The daemon's state owns
the producer allocations; the returned tensors and records keep the
consumer's imported mappings alive. A connected engine does not need the
daemon to stay alive to retain those mappings, but future engines need a
live daemon to fetch them. Zero-copy loading works with either backend.
Same-host peer seeding detects VMM storage and transfers its
descriptors; remote seeding continues to use its configured copy mover.
VMM uses the existing FD transport and D2D protocols; this PR does not
rewrite either one.

Both backends can be deployed independently of the serving Pod, provided
GPU access, socket mounts, ownership and container settings allow it.
VMM does not introduce that deployment capability. The daemon still
loads the model and initializes the GPU; this backend does not implement
a separate GPU-free FD holder. It also does not add EPLB or sleep-mode
support.

The built-in `torch_ipc` and `vmm_ipc` backends are the only statically
supported backends. They implement
|vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.ipc_backends.base.IPCBackendBase|,
whose `materialize(storages)` returns `StorageRecord`s and whose
`export_storage(record)` returns a rebuildable `StorageEntry`. Tensor
reconstruction remains separate from the model's processed-weight
contract and daemon-to-daemon copy movers.

             *features-preload-index-preloading-the-flashinfer-autotune-cache*
PRELOADING THE FLASHINFER AUTOTUNE CACHE

FlashInfer has several implementations of each operation and chooses
between them by benchmarking. vLLM runs that pass during kernel warmup
and persists the result per rank in the on-disk FlashInfer autotune
cache (under `VLLM_CACHE_ROOT`, or
`VLLM_FLASHINFER_AUTOTUNE_CACHE_DIR`), so a later engine with the same
configuration loads the tuned configs instead of profiling again.
Normally the first serving engine pays for the pass, and on a large MoE
model it dominates what is left of the startup time once the weights
come from the daemon.

Unless FlashInfer autotune is disabled
(`--disable-flashinfer-autotune`), `vllm preload` pays it at preload
time: each daemon runs the autotune pass in place --- profiling dummy
runs against the shard it already holds --- after loading and before
binding its socket, then writes the cache:

>bash
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 4
<
After warmup and state capture, the daemon releases its communication
resources and, on CUDA, cached cuBLAS workspaces before accepting engine
connections. The state's storages remain resident for sharing through
the selected backend. This reduces the daemon's GPU memory overhead; the
amount reclaimed depends on the model and allocator state.

            *features-preload-index-seeding-warmup-caches-between-daemons-d2d*
Seeding warmup caches between daemons (D2D)

A fresh daemon replica --- a new node joining a fleet, a restarted
preload set --- would normally pay the autotune pass itself. With a seed
source configured, each daemon rank fetches its rank-matched warmup
caches from the source replica over an authenticated TCP control plane
before tuning, validates them, and installs them into its own on-disk
cache, so its warmup skips the profiling sweep. Any failure
(unreachable, missing, incompatible) falls back to local tuning.

Source replica:

>bash
    MOONCAKE_CONFIG_PATH=/path/to/mooncake_config.json \
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 4 \
        --seed-listen-host 0.0.0.0 --seed-listen-port 29600 \
        --seed-token "$TOKEN" --warmup-cache-stores mooncake
<
Mirror replica (possibly on another node):

>bash
    MOONCAKE_CONFIG_PATH=/path/to/mooncake_config.json \
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 4 \
        --seed-source-host <source-host> --seed-source-port 29600 \
        --seed-token "$TOKEN" --warmup-cache-stores mooncake
<
Each rank binds and dials `base_port + group offset + its global rank`,
so one `host:base_port` pair covers a whole single-node replica,
including the draft daemon group when speculative decoding is
configured. The standalone `--seed-source-host` option names one source
host, so it cannot by itself reach every node of a multi-node source.
When mirroring weights from a multi-node source, the per-node
`--weight-cache-seed` addresses below also provide per-node warmup cache
sources. The Store options in these examples enable compiled-cache
reuse; autotune-only D2D seeding does not require them.

Compiled-cache reuse requires a live D2D source and the same Store
configuration on source and target. After warmup, the source publishes a
content-addressed compiled bundle as
`vllm-warmup-cache/compile_cache/<bundle-sha256>/bundle.tar`. Its
authenticated D2D response carries the cache manifest, bundle key, byte
length and SHA256 digest, not the bundle itself. The target fetches the
bundle from the Store, verifies its length and digest, and installs it
before model loading. A Store miss or error, an invalid or incompatible
bundle, or a D2D failure falls back to local compilation. A Store
without a source cannot provide a compiled-cache cold start.

The source scans compiled caches only when a shared Store is configured.
If collecting or publishing the bundle fails, for example because the
cache exceeds the 8 GiB limit, the source logs a warning and continues
serving model weights. It advertises no compiled bundle, so targets use
local compilation. Invalid Store configuration and Store initialization
errors remain startup errors.

For autotune tables, the exchange is keyed by the on-disk cache layout:
a request names the cache by kind, configuration hash, DP rank and world
rank, and the source resolves it to files under its cache root. Before
installing, the target rejects a payload whose FlashInfer environment
metadata (GPU, CUDA/cuDNN stack) differs from its own --- such a table
would never load anyway, and a hard conflict is never overwritten, which
would poison the cache file into re-tuning on every start. Tables are
shareable across vLLM versions as long as the configuration hash
matches.

The channel is a generic seeding point: warmup caches are the first
kind, and further daemon state (e.g. Triton warmup caches or weight
manifests) plugs in as additional kinds. Small JSON payloads and bundle
descriptors travel over the authenticated control plane; compiled
bundles are fetched separately from the configured shared Store.

                       *features-preload-index-seeding-a-replica-from-another*
SEEDING A REPLICA FROM ANOTHER

A second replica's daemons can fill their shards from a replica that is
already loaded, instead of reading the checkpoint again. A mirror daemon
is an ordinary weight cache daemon: it forms its own world group and
rendezvous like any preload (the multi-node `--weight-cache-master-port`
requirements apply unchanged) and builds the model itself, so the
model's config files must be present locally --- only the safetensors
are never read. What changes is where the weights come from: the
daemon's loader pulls the post-processed, TP-sharded weights directly
from its rank-matched source daemon. The FlashInfer autotune table does
not travel with the weights; instead each mirror daemon adopts it from
the same source through the ordinary
|features-preload-index-seeding-warmup-caches-between-daemons-d2d| path
--- with `--weight-cache-seed` set and no explicit `--seed-source-*`,
each daemon automatically seeds its warmup caches from its weights
source, so the new replica's first engine also skips the autotune pass.
If the source has no cache to send (autotune disabled, or the single-GPU
managed store), the mirror tunes in place and fills its local cache
instead, like any other daemon.

The address form selects the mover: Unix socket paths copy through CUDA
IPC on one host (`peer_ipc`):

>bash
    # source replica on GPUs 0-3, already serving
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 4 \
        --weight-cache-socket-dir /run/vllm-a

    # mirror replica, filled from the source (it lands on the free GPUs 4-7)
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 4 \
        --weight-cache-socket-dir /run/vllm-b \
        --weight-cache-seed /run/vllm-a/vllm_weight_cache_<hash0>.sock,/run/vllm-a/vllm_weight_cache_<hash1>.sock,/run/vllm-a/vllm_weight_cache_<hash2>.sock,/run/vllm-a/vllm_weight_cache_<hash3>.sock
<
`--weight-cache-seed` takes one source target socket per local GPU, in
device order, and rank i of the mirror is filled from rank i of the
source --- the cache fingerprints are compared first, so a mismatched
pairing is rejected rather than silently mirroring the wrong shard. With
speculative decoding, always pass the target sockets; each draft daemon
automatically uses the corresponding `*_draft.sock` path instead. The
socket paths live in the source's `--weight-cache-socket-dir` and are
named from each GPU's UUID, as described under
|features-preload-index-loader-configuration|. The mirror fetches each
source rank's manifest over that same owner-verified Unix socket, so no
token or network listener is involved. A mirror on a host without the
checkpoint's safetensors cannot fingerprint them, so it matches the
source by `--model` path instead, as an engine without the files matches
its daemon; give both sides the same `--model` in that case.

D2D seeding copies each source storage once, transferring only the
logical `nbytes` prefix recorded in the manifest rather than the full
physical allocation. The mirror then calls `rebuild_tensors` to rebuild
every view at its recorded offset and strides, so views that
post-processing leaves on one storage (such as MLA's `W_UV` and
`W_UK_T`) keep their layout and stay shared.

A same-host mirror keeps every GPU visible --- a CUDA IPC handle names
its device by index, so the source's indices must resolve --- and the
launcher places the mirror's ranks on the GPUs the source does not use:
it learns the source's GPU UUIDs from its sockets and takes the
remaining indices in order, so no placement flag is needed and an
arbitrary source placement (not just a leading block) works. Do not
remap devices with `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES`; the mirror refuses a handle
that would land on the wrong physical GPU.

`rdma` pulls through the Mooncake TransferEngine and is the mover for a
source on another host. The source has to expose its daemon-to-daemon
listener, and both sides need the same shared token:

>bash
    # single-node source host
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 8 \
        --seed-listen-host 0.0.0.0 --seed-listen-port 29700 \
        --seed-token "$TOKEN"

    # single-node mirror host
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 8 \
        --weight-cache-seed 10.0.0.1:29700 --seed-token "$TOKEN"
<
For a multi-node source, pass one `host:base_port` per source node,
comma separated in `node_rank` order (for example,
`10.0.0.1:29700,10.0.0.2:29700`). A single `host:base_port` remains the
single-node form. The source and mirror must be homogeneous: the same
node count, parallel layout and rank placement, so every mirror rank has
a rank-matched source. Each mirror node selects the address at its own
`node_rank`, then each rank dials
`base_port + group offset + its global rank`. The draft daemon group's
listeners sit past the target group's port block on every source node,
so the selected address seeds the draft group too. An IPv6 source is
written `[address]:base_port`. Do not mix remote addresses with Unix
socket paths in one list. The remote form pulls over Mooncake RDMA and
requires `--seed-token`.

  Warning: ~

  The remote control plane accepts JSON seed requests after
  authenticating the shared token; it never uses pickle. The connection
  is unencrypted, so use it only on a trusted network and keep the token
  secret. Exporting IPC handles remains a local operation over the
  owner-verified Unix socket.

      *features-preload-index-sharing-warmup-caches-through-mooncake-optional*
SHARING WARMUP CACHES THROUGH MOONCAKE (OPTIONAL)

For autotune tables, pluggable shared stores are an alternative to D2D
seeding and let a new node fetch them even when no source replica is
online. `--warmup-cache-stores` takes a comma-separated list of store
backends; the `mooncake` backend uses a Mooncake
https://github.com/kvcache-ai/Mooncake distributed store. Point
`MOONCAKE_CONFIG_PATH` at the same JSON configuration used by
|features-mooncake_store_connector_usage-index-configure-mooncake|:

>bash
    MOONCAKE_CONFIG_PATH=/path/to/mooncake_config.json \
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 4 \
        --warmup-cache-stores mooncake
<
After tuning, each daemon publishes its per-rank autotune table to every
configured store under keys of the form
`vllm-warmup-cache/<kind>/<config-hash>/<filename>`, in addition to
writing its local disk cache. All tiers share one interface: the local
disk is always tier zero, and the stores listed in
`--warmup-cache-stores` follow it in list order --- reads take the first
tier that hits, writes fan out to all of them. During warmup a daemon
pulls its table from the shared stores first, in list order; when no
store has it --- or none are configured --- it falls back to D2D seeding
from a live source daemon, and finally tunes in place. Stores come first
because they are built for read fan-out, while a D2D fetch makes a busy
peer proxy the same bytes. For autotune tables, this covers a cold start
with no live source daemon and nodes whose cache root is ephemeral. When
serving peers, a daemon answers from the same chain: disk first, then
the stores. Whatever it fetches is validated and installed to the local
disk cache as usual. A misspelled store name or a store that cannot be
configured fails daemon startup immediately; at runtime, store
operations are best-effort and never block warmup.

Prefer the `standalone-store` topology for this use case. In `embedded`
mode every daemon contributes `global_segment_size` of host memory to
the pool, which is hard to justify for kilobyte-sized JSON artifacts.
Note that the store is a trust boundary: any writer in the Mooncake
cluster can influence the autotune tables and compiled bundles other
nodes load, so only point daemons at a trusted deployment.

HOW IT WORKS                             *features-preload-index-how-it-works*

1. `vllm preload` spawns one daemon process per GPU. Each daemon loads
   and post-processes its shard, runs warmup, then publishes a
   |vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.protocol.WeightCacheState|
   containing a manifest of the final canonical parameters and buffers
   and their storage records through the selected sharing backend (CUDA
   IPC by default). It drops the model and startup runtime resources
   before accepting engine connections.
2. An engine started with `--load-format ipc_cache` builds its model on
   the meta device (no storage) and requests the storage entries and
   state metadata from the daemon on its GPU. It rebuilds each entry
   into a `StorageRecord`, constructs
   |vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.protocol.WeightCacheState|,
   then uses the manifest to recreate all tensor views.
3. Before serving anything, the engine and daemon compare a fingerprint
   of the cached weights: checkpoint content (hashed from safetensors
   metadata, so identical weights in different directories still match),
   model architecture, TP/DP size/rank, dtype, quantization method and
   config, text RoPE parameters, model revision, KV cache dtype and
   quantization skip layers, the multimodal options that skip model
   components, and vLLM version. On any mismatch the engine falls back
   to disk loading (unless `fallback` is disabled).
4. The engine also verifies the daemon's GPU UUID matches its own
   device, so a stale socket cannot serve weights for the wrong GPU.

Tied weights (e.g. `lm_head.weight` sharing storage with
`embed_tokens.weight`) share a storage export; the manifest and aliases
re-establish their views and parameter identity in the engine.

Shared weights count toward the engine's `gpu_memory_utilization`
budget. If IPC loading falls back to disk, the engine removes the
corresponding external weight charge before profiling its locally loaded
weights for KV cache sizing. The updated budget must still fit the
available GPU memory. Engines never actively release the daemon's cache,
and fallback does not stop the daemon: its cached weights remain
resident until the daemon shuts down.

                      *features-preload-index-multi-node-and-data-parallelism*
MULTI-NODE AND DATA PARALLELISM

Local backend handles are node-local, so each node serves only its local
GPUs' shards. For multi-node tensor parallelism, run one `vllm preload`
launcher per node with a shared rendezvous: reuse the `--nnodes` /
`--node-rank` / `--master-addr` flags you pass the engine, plus a
`--weight-cache-master-port` distinct from the engine's `--master-port`:

>bash
    # node 0 (8 local GPUs)
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 16 \
        --nnodes 2 --node-rank 0 --master-addr 10.0.0.1 \
        --weight-cache-master-port 29600
    # node 1 (8 local GPUs)
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 16 \
        --nnodes 2 --node-rank 1 --master-addr 10.0.0.1 \
        --weight-cache-master-port 29600
<
For data parallelism (e.g. a TP1 x DP16 x EP decode fleet), run one
launcher per node with the engine's DP placement flags. Local GPU `i`
serves DP rank `start_rank + i // tp_size` and TP rank `i % tp_size`,
and all `dp_size * tp_size` daemons form one world group on
`--data-parallel-address` / `--weight-cache-master-port` so the expert
shards are laid out exactly as in the engine:

>bash
    # node r (4 local GPUs)
    vllm preload --model /path/to/model --tensor-parallel-size 1 \
        --enable-expert-parallel \
        --data-parallel-size 16 --data-parallel-size-local 4 \
        --data-parallel-start-rank 4r --data-parallel-address 10.0.0.1 \
        --weight-cache-master-port 29600
<
Data parallelism also combines with multi-node tensor parallelism: pass
both flag sets. Each node then serves a contiguous block of the
`dp_size * tp_size` global ranks.

SPECULATIVE DECODING             *features-preload-index-speculative-decoding*

With MTP, EAGLE or EAGLE3 speculative decoding, `vllm preload`
additionally starts a draft daemon group that caches the draft model. It
uses its own cache key, Unix sockets (`*_draft.sock`) and rendezvous
port (`--weight-cache-draft-master-port`, default
`--weight-cache-master-port + 1`), so each daemon process serves exactly
one model role. Other draft types are not cached and keep loading from
disk in the engine. With pipeline parallelism, only the GPUs of the last
stage serve the draft model, since the engine runs the draft model
there; draft daemons on the other stages exit after startup.

LOADER CONFIGURATION             *features-preload-index-loader-configuration*

The `ipc_cache` loader supports only zero-copy loading of new model
instances; standalone in-place weight reload is not supported. The
engine maps the daemon's shared allocations directly through the
selected backend, so each GPU carries only one copy of the weights.
Already-mapped engines keep serving if the daemon exits, but a restarted
daemon holds a second copy and new engines cannot attach until it is
ready.

The loader accepts extra keys via `--model-loader-extra-config`:

>bash
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \
        --load-format ipc_cache \
        --model-loader-extra-config '{"fallback": false}'
<

Key                Default                       Description
-----------------  ----------------------------  -----------------------------
socket_path        per-GPU path derived from     Explicit daemon socket path.
                   the GPU UUID
socket_dir         per-user private dir under    Directory containing the
                   the temp dir                  daemon sockets.
fallback           true                          Fall back to disk loading
                                                 when the daemon is
                                                 unavailable or the
                                                 fingerprints mismatch.
connect_timeout_s  5.0                           Socket connect timeout in
                                                 seconds.
state_timeout_s    300.0                         Timeout for the
                                                 weight-transfer request in
                                                 seconds.

The daemon's `--weight-cache-socket-dir` and the loader's `socket_dir` /
`socket_path` must agree when the default per-user directory is not
used. Socket paths are derived from the GPU UUID, so they are stable
regardless of `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` index remapping.

  Warning: ~

  The weights live in externally held backend allocations, so
  |vllm-features-sleep_mode-index| (CuMemAllocator weight offloading)
  must not be used with this loader.

LIMITATIONS                               *features-preload-index-limitations*

- Platform: CUDA and ROCm only; other platforms raise
  |vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.protocol.UnsupportedPlatformForIPCError|
  even when `fallback` is enabled, since it is a permanent
  misconfiguration rather than a transient daemon outage.
- Parallelism: tensor, expert, pipeline and data parallelism are
  supported, including across nodes.
- Autotune preloading: tuning runs across the daemon's world group,
  including across nodes and DP ranks. With model-based speculative
  decoding both daemon groups skip it (the target cannot run the
  drafter's dummy passes, and a table tuned on the draft model would key
  on the draft's config, which no engine looks up), so those
  deployments' first engine tunes and fills the cache instead.
- Cache seeding: autotune seeding supports only the v1 per-rank
  FlashInfer cache; single-rank daemons use FlashInfer's managed v2
  store and always tune in place. Compiled-cache seeding additionally
  requires a live D2D source and matching Store configuration on source
  and target; a Store alone cannot cold-start compiled caches. Seeding
  requires a trusted network --- the TCP channel is authenticated by a
  shared token but unencrypted. Shared stores can serve autotune tables
  without a live source. Cache misses and errors fall back to local
  tuning or compilation.
- Weight seeding: `peer_ipc` cannot cross hosts, because local sharing
  handles are node-local; `rdma` requires Mooncake on both sides. A
  mirror copies the weights into its own memory, so it does not depend
  on the source staying alive afterwards, and a mirror replica forms its
  own world group like a normal preload. Each outstanding copy holds a
  lease until the mirror acknowledges it. During graceful shutdown, the
  source waits for these acknowledgements before weight memory is freed,
  subject to the timeout under
  |features-preload-index-daemon-lifecycle|.
- Autotuning cannot overlap the load: autotuning picks a tactic by
  timing candidates, so running it while a daemon is still copying
  weights onto the same GPU would measure the wrong thing and cache the
  wrong choice. To pay the download once per machine instead, prefetch
  FlashInfer's kernels separately with
  `python -m flashinfer download-cubin` (it fetches the whole cubin set,
  so do it when building an image, not on a serving node).
- Quantization: every quantization method in the model must declare
  support for pre-processed weights (the daemon transfers weights after
  quantization post-processing). Unsupported methods raise
  |vllm.model_executor.model_loader.weight_cache.protocol.UnsupportedQuantForIPCError|.
  Online `fp8_per_tensor`, `fp8_per_channel`, and `fp8_per_block` dense
  linear layers support CUDA IPC with the CUTLASS linear backend. Set
  `--kernel-config '{"linear_backend": "cutlass"}'` on both the daemon
  and engine; the block variant additionally requires a GPU supported by
  the CUTLASS block-FP8 kernel. Online `mxfp8` linear layers can reuse
  weights with an MXFP8 kernel that supports pre-processed weights,
  including FlashInfer CUTLASS on Blackwell. The engine reuses the
  quantized weights and scales without running online quantization
  again. This does not enable online MoE quantization or conversion from
  an already quantized checkpoint. Serialized `compressed-tensors` W8A8
  FP8 dense linear layers support per-tensor and per-channel weights
  with static per-tensor or dynamic per-token activations when both
  processes select the CUTLASS linear backend. Engines retain the
  processed tensor layout and scales and rebuild attention scale
  metadata from the cached scale buffers. Other compressed-tensors
  schemes, transform wrappers, block FP8 and MoE are not enabled by this
  support. Serialized ModelOpt `FP8` (per-tensor) and
  `FP8_PER_CHANNEL_PER_TOKEN` linear layers also support CUDA IPC with
  the CUTLASS linear backend. Set the same
  `--kernel-config '{"linear_backend": "cutlass"}'` on the daemon and
  engine. Engines reuse the processed weights and scales, including
  fused-projection scales and output padding. This does not enable
  ModelOpt block FP8, FP8 MoE, or other FP8 linear backends. Serialized
  ModelOpt `INT8_PB_WO` linear layers can also reuse pre-processed
  weights with the Marlin backend and FP16/BF16 activations. Humming and
  ModelOpt INT8 MoE layers are not covered. Serialized
  `compressed-tensors` W8A8 FP8 linear layers with 128×128 weight blocks
  and dynamic per-token group-128 activations support CUDA IPC when both
  processes select the CUTLASS block-FP8 backend on a supported GPU. Set
  `--kernel-config '{"linear_backend": "cutlass"}'` on the daemon and
  engine. Attached weights retain their untransposed layout. Other
  compressed-tensors schemes, block sizes, transform wrappers, linear
  backends and MoE are not enabled by this support. Serialized
  `compressed-tensors` WNA16 linear layers can reuse Marlin-packed
  weights with FP16/BF16 activations. Humming, other WNA16 backends and
  MoE are not enabled by this support. ModelOpt NVFP4 MoE supports the
  TRT-LLM and vLLM CUTLASS backends. With CUTLASS, the daemon fuses
  activation scales once; engines reuse the fused scales without
  modifying the daemon's shared tensors.
- Consistency: the daemon and engine must run the same vLLM version and
  agree on model, dtype, quantization, and TP/DP layout, otherwise the
  fingerprint mismatch triggers the disk fallback. For multimodal
  models, also pass the same `--language-model-only`,
  `--mm-encoder-only`, and zero `--limit-mm-per-prompt` entries to both:
  they decide which components (such as the vision tower) are built.
  Nonzero limits may differ.
- Localhost only: the daemon serves over a Unix domain socket; both the
  daemon and the engines must run on the same node as the same user.

SECURITY                                     *features-preload-index-security*

The socket protocol uses pickle and is intended only for trusted local
processes owned by the same user:

- Daemon sockets live in a per-user private directory (mode `0700`) and
  the socket files are restricted to the owner (`0600`).
- Both sides reject symlinked or non-owned socket paths; the
  auto-derived directory is additionally rejected if it is group/world
  accessible.
- On Linux the daemon verifies the connecting peer's UID via
  `SO_PEERCRED`.

The optional daemon-to-daemon listener (`--seed-listen-*`) is a separate
TCP channel: requests are JSON-only and size-capped, authenticate with
the shared `--seed-token` (constant-time comparison), and an
unauthenticated peer that stops sending is cut off by a whole-message
deadline instead of blocking the accept loop. The channel is unencrypted
--- bind it to a trusted network.

The optional shared stores (`--warmup-cache-stores`) trust the store
they are pointed at: a writer in the store's cluster can influence the
autotune tables and compiled bundles a daemon installs, so use them only
with a trusted deployment.

See the |vllm-usage-security-index| for vLLM's general threat model.

DAEMON LIFECYCLE                     *features-preload-index-daemon-lifecycle*

- Each GPU's socket path is guarded by an exclusive lock file, so a
  second daemon for the same GPU fails fast instead of clobbering the
  live socket.
- `vllm preload` reports readiness once every rank is serving. If any
  rank exits during startup or serving, the launcher terminates and
  reaps the remaining ranks, stops its health endpoint, and exits with a
  nonzero status. The failing rank's nonzero exit code is preserved.
  Signal failures use `128 + signal number` (for example, 137 for
  `SIGKILL`); an unexpected clean rank exit is reported as status 1.
  Restart the entire preload group before attaching new engines.
- `SIGINT`/`SIGTERM` to the `vllm preload` process terminates all daemon
  ranks and exits with status 130/143.
- During graceful shutdown, a daemon serving as a D2D weight source
  stops accepting new copy leases and waits up to five minutes for
  outstanding copies to acknowledge before releasing its weight memory.
  The launcher allows 30 seconds beyond this lease timeout before
  killing and reaping ranks that have not stopped. When shutdown follows
  a rank failure, the launcher instead allows ten seconds. A forcibly
  terminated rank may leave a stale socket; the next daemon replaces it
  after acquiring the per-GPU lock.

See |vllm-cli-preload-index| for the full CLI reference.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Prompt Embedding Inputs                    *vllm-features-prompt_embeds-index*

This page teaches you how to pass prompt embedding inputs to vLLM.

                     *features-prompt_embeds-index-what-are-prompt-embeddings*
WHAT ARE PROMPT EMBEDDINGS?

The traditional flow of text data for a Large Language Model goes from
text to token ids (via a tokenizer) then from token ids to prompt
embeddings. For a traditional decoder-only model (such as
meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct), this step of converting token ids to
prompt embeddings happens via a look-up from a learned embedding matrix,
but the model is not limited to processing only the embeddings
corresponding to its token vocabulary.

OFFLINE INFERENCE             *features-prompt_embeds-index-offline-inference*

To input multi-modal data, follow this schema in
|vllm.inputs.EmbedsPrompt|:

- `prompt_embeds`: A torch tensor representing a sequence of
  prompt/token embeddings. This has the shape (sequence_length,
  hidden_size), where sequence length is the number of tokens embeddings
  and hidden_size is the hidden size (embedding size) of the model.

               *features-prompt_embeds-index-hugging-face-transformers-inputs*
Hugging Face Transformers Inputs

You can pass prompt embeddings from Hugging Face Transformers models to
the `'prompt_embeds'` field of the prompt embedding dictionary, as shown
in the following examples:

examples/features/prompt_embed/prompt_embed_offline.py
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/main/examples/features/prompt_embed/prompt_embed_offline.py

ONLINE SERVING                   *features-prompt_embeds-index-online-serving*

Our OpenAI-compatible server accepts prompt embeddings inputs via both
the Completions API
https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/completions and the
Chat Completions API
https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/chat. Both are enabled by
the `--enable-prompt-embeds` flag in `vllm serve`.

Completions API                 *features-prompt_embeds-index-completions-api*

Prompt embeddings inputs are added via a `'prompt_embeds'` key in the
JSON request body.

When a mixture of `'prompt_embeds'` and `'prompt'` inputs are provided
in a single request, the prompt embeds are always returned first.

Prompt embeddings are passed in as base64 encoded torch tensors.

The Completions endpoint does not apply a chat template to
`prompt_embeds`. If the model assumes some chat template, the caller is
responsible for producing embeddings for the full, already-templated
prompt: apply the chat template, then embed the resulting token IDs.
Anything the model would normally need (system prompt, role markers,
generation prompt, etc.) must already be baked into the embedded tokens.

Chat Completions API       *features-prompt_embeds-index-chat-completions-api*

Prompt embeddings can be included as content parts in chat messages,
interleaved with text:

>json
    {
      "messages": [
        {
          "role": "system",
          "content": [
            {"type": "text", "text": "You are a helpful assistant."},
            {"type": "prompt_embeds", "data": "<base64_encoded_tensor>"}
          ]
        },
        {
          "role": "user",
          "content": [
            {"type": "prompt_embeds", "data": "<base64_encoded_tensor>"},
            {"type": "text", "text": "Summarize the above."}
          ]
        }
      ]
    }
<
Each `prompt_embeds` content part contains a `data` field with a
base64-encoded `torch.Tensor` of shape `(num_tokens, hidden_size)`.
Multiple `prompt_embeds` parts can appear in any message, in any
position relative to text parts. The server expands each part into the
correct number of placeholder tokens during chat template rendering,
then splices the pre-computed embeddings into the model's input at the
corresponding positions.

For multimodal models, `prompt_embeds` parts can also be mixed with
image content parts. Their placeholder token IDs are replaced with safe
dummy IDs before model execution; the supplied embeddings replace the
dummy embeddings at those positions.

Unlike the Completions API, a `prompt_embeds` content part should encode
only the content, not a templated conversation. The server wraps the
chat template around the embedded content at request time, the same way
it would for a plain text `content` string. Embedding a full templated
conversation here would double-apply the template and produce incorrect
inputs to the model.

Assistant messages can also pass a single `prompt_embeds` part as
`reasoning` instead of a string:

>json
    {
      "messages": [
        {
          "role": "assistant",
          "reasoning": {"type": "prompt_embeds", "data": "<base64_encoded_tensor>"},
          "content": ""
        }
      ]
    }
<
The tensor represents only the reasoning content. Use a chat template
that renders `reasoning` (or its legacy alias `reasoning_content`); the
server inserts the reasoning delimiters through the template. This uses
the existing Hugging Face chat-template rendering path. The same
`--enable-prompt-embeds` flag and tensor shape requirements apply.

  Warning: ~

  The vLLM engine may crash if incorrect shape of embeddings is passed.
  Only enable this flag for trusted users!

          *features-prompt_embeds-index-transformers-inputs-via-openai-client*
Transformers Inputs via OpenAI Client

First, launch the OpenAI-compatible server:

>bash
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct --runner generate \
      --max-model-len 4096 --enable-prompt-embeds
<
Then, you can use the OpenAI client as follows:

examples/features/prompt_embed/prompt_embed_inference_with_openai_client.py
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/main/examples/features/prompt_embed/prompt_embed_inference_with_openai_client.py

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
AutoAWQ                                   *vllm-quantization-auto_awq-index-2*

  ⚠️ Warning: The `AutoAWQ` library is deprecated. This functionality
  has been adopted by the vLLM project in `llm-compressor`
  https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/tree/main/examples/awq.
  For the recommended quantization workflow, please see the AWQ examples
  in `llm-compressor`
  https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/tree/main/examples/awq.
  For more details on the deprecation, refer to the original
  AutoAWQ repository https://github.com/casper-hansen/AutoAWQ.

To create a new 4-bit quantized model, you can leverage AutoAWQ
https://github.com/casper-hansen/AutoAWQ. Quantization reduces the
model's precision from BF16/FP16 to INT4 which effectively reduces the
total model memory footprint. The main benefits are lower latency and
memory usage.

You can quantize your own models by installing AutoAWQ or picking one of
the 6500+ models on Huggingface
https://huggingface.co/models?search=awq.

>bash
    pip install autoawq
<
After installing AutoAWQ, you are ready to quantize a model. Please
refer to the AutoAWQ documentation
https://casper-hansen.github.io/AutoAWQ/examples/#basic-quantization for
further details. Here is an example of how to quantize
`mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2`:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from awq import AutoAWQForCausalLM
      from transformers import AutoTokenizer

      model_path = "mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2"
      quant_path = "mistral-instruct-v0.2-awq"
      quant_config = {"zero_point": True, "q_group_size": 128, "w_bit": 4, "version": "GEMM"}

      # Load model
      model = AutoAWQForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
          model_path,
          low_cpu_mem_usage=True,
          use_cache=False,
      )
      tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_path, trust_remote_code=True)

      # Quantize
      model.quantize(tokenizer, quant_config=quant_config)

      # Save quantized model
      model.save_quantized(quant_path)
      tokenizer.save_pretrained(quant_path)

      print(f'Model is quantized and saved at "{quant_path}"')
<

To run an AWQ model with vLLM, you can use TheBloke/Llama-2-7b-Chat-AWQ
https://huggingface.co/TheBloke/Llama-2-7b-Chat-AWQ with the following
command:

>bash
    python examples/deployment/llm_engine_example.py \
        --model TheBloke/Llama-2-7b-Chat-AWQ \
        --quantization auto_awq
<
AWQ models are also supported directly through the LLM entrypoint:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

      # Sample prompts.
      prompts = [
          "Hello, my name is",
          "The president of the United States is",
          "The capital of France is",
          "The future of AI is",
      ]
      # Create a sampling params object.
      sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.8, top_p=0.95)

      # Create an LLM.
      llm = LLM(model="TheBloke/Llama-2-7b-Chat-AWQ", quantization="auto_awq")
      # Generate texts from the prompts. The output is a list of RequestOutput objects
      # that contain the prompt, generated text, and other information.
      outputs = llm.generate(prompts, sampling_params)
      # Print the outputs.
      for output in outputs:
          prompt = output.prompt
          generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
          print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}, Generated text: {generated_text!r}")
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
b12x Linear and MoE Backends                    *vllm-quantization-b12x-index*

b12x provides CUDA kernels for NVIDIA SM120 and SM121 GPUs. It ships
with FlashInfer (`flashinfer-python>=0.7.2`), so no separate install is
needed.

b12x linear kernels participate in automatic selection after established
optimized backends and before emulation. Select the linear and MoE
backends explicitly with:

>bash
    vllm serve <model> \
        --linear-backend b12x \
        --moe-backend b12x
<
Only pass `--moe-backend b12x` for a compatible packed-weight MoE model.
The linear and MoE backends can be selected independently.

b12x uses MXFP8 activations by default for MXFP4 MoE and the
checkpoint's activation format for NVFP4 MoE. MXFP4 falls back to BF16
when its A8 path does not support the model configuration. Set
`VLLM_B12X_MOE_FP4_FORCE_A16=1` to force BF16 activations for either FP4
weight format.

SUPPORTED CONFIGURATIONS    *quantization-b12x-index-supported-configurations*

Backend    Supported configurations
---------  -------------------------------------------------------------------
Linear     Per-tensor FP8, 128x128 block FP8, MXFP8, NVFP4 (including W4A16),
           MXFP4, IQ2_XS, IQ2_XXS, and Q8_0
MoE        Tensor-parallel MXFP4 weights with BF16 or MXFP8 activations; NVFP4
           weights with BF16, NVFP4, or MXFP8 activations; IQ2_XS, IQ2_XXS,
           and Q8_0 weights with BF16 activations
Embedding  Packed Q8_0 lookup with BF16 output

The b12x MoE backend does not support expert parallelism, expert maps,
EXL3, or NF3.

MODELOPT BLOCK CODECS          *quantization-b12x-index-modelopt-block-codecs*

ModelOpt mixed-precision checkpoints select block codecs through the
`quantization_config.quantized_layers` entries in `config.json`. Each
entry declares `quant_algo`, `group_size`, `block_payload_bytes`, and
`packing="ggml"`:

quant_algo  group_size  block_payload_bytes
----------  ----------  ------------------------------------------------------
IQ2_XS      256         74
IQ2_XXS     256         66
Q8_0        32          34

Serialized matrices have uint8 shape
`[N, K/group_size, block_payload_bytes]`. Dense preparation rearranges
the encoded blocks without expanding the matrix to BF16. Routed experts
use bias-free SiLU or non-gated ReLU²; tensor-parallel shards must
preserve whole blocks and satisfy the kernel's alignment constraints.
Only Q8_0 supports packed embedding lookup.

Quantized normalization weights and other packed vectors are not
supported. Store normalization weights as ordinary floating-point
tensors and omit their entries from
`quantization_config.quantized_layers`.

Dense, embedding, and expert matrices remain packed. Their execution
uses BF16 activations without activation quantization. Prepare the
kernels and workspaces before CUDA graph capture.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
BitsAndBytes                                     *vllm-quantization-bnb-index*

vLLM now supports BitsAndBytes
https://github.com/TimDettmers/bitsandbytes for more efficient model
inference. BitsAndBytes quantizes models to reduce memory usage and
enhance performance without significantly sacrificing accuracy. Compared
to other quantization methods, BitsAndBytes eliminates the need for
calibrating the quantized model with input data.

BitsAndBytes support is provided by the out-of-tree `vllm-bnb-plugin`
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-bnb-plugin. Install the plugin
before using BitsAndBytes with vLLM:

>bash
    uv pip install vllm-bnb-plugin
<
vLLM reads the model's config file and supports both in-flight
quantization and pre-quantized checkpoint.

You can find bitsandbytes quantized models on Hugging Face
https://huggingface.co/models?search=bitsandbytes. And usually, these
repositories have a config.json file that includes a quantization_config
section.

READ QUANTIZED CHECKPOINT   *quantization-bnb-index-read-quantized-checkpoint*

For pre-quantized checkpoints, vLLM will try to infer the quantization
method from the config file, so you don't need to explicitly specify the
quantization argument.

>python
    from vllm import LLM
    import torch
    # unsloth/tinyllama-bnb-4bit is a pre-quantized checkpoint.
    model_id = "unsloth/tinyllama-bnb-4bit"
    llm = LLM(
        model=model_id,
        dtype=torch.bfloat16,
        trust_remote_code=True,
    )
<

      *quantization-bnb-index-inflight-quantization-load-as-4bit-quantization*
INFLIGHT QUANTIZATION: LOAD AS 4BIT QUANTIZATION

For inflight 4bit quantization with BitsAndBytes, you need to explicitly
specify the quantization argument.

>python
    from vllm import LLM
    import torch
    model_id = "huggyllama/llama-7b"
    llm = LLM(
        model=model_id,
        dtype=torch.bfloat16,
        trust_remote_code=True,
        quantization="bitsandbytes",
    )
<

OPENAI COMPATIBLE SERVER     *quantization-bnb-index-openai-compatible-server*

Append the following to your model arguments for 4bit inflight
quantization:

>bash
    --quantization bitsandbytes
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Methods and Schemes                          *vllm-quantization-formats-index*

This page defines the terms the quantization pages use and lists the
names vLLM accepts for quantization methods and schemes.

The tables are generated at docs build time by
`docs/mkdocs/gen_files/generate_quantization_tables.py` from the
registries in `vllm/config/quantization.py` and
`vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization/` and from the platform
allow-lists. A name added to a registry appears here on the next build.
Names registered by
|features-quantization-index-out-of-tree-quantization-plugins| are not
listed.

TERMS                                       *quantization-formats-index-terms*

Term                 Meaning                      Where it appears
-------------------  ---------------------------  ----------------------------
Method               The implementation that      quant_method in the model's
                     loads a quantized model.     config.json, or
                     Most methods read one        --quantization
                     checkpoint format and are
                     named after the toolkit
                     that writes it, e.g.
                     compressed-tensors,
                     modelopt, quark.
Online quantization  Quantizing full-precision    --quantization <shorthand>,
                     weights while the model      --quantization-config
                     loads.
Scheme               The numeric format of one    The weight and activation
                     tensor: its data format,     fields of
                     its scale format, and what   --quantization-config
                     shares a scale.
Kernel               The code that runs a layer   --linear-backend,
                     for a given weight and       --moe-backend; see Kernel
                     activation scheme.           Backends

A method names how a model is loaded; a scheme names the format of one
tensor. One checkpoint can hold layers with different schemes, and
kernels are selected per layer from the scheme.

`W<n>A<m>` gives the bits of weights and activations: W4A16 is 4-bit
weights with 16-bit activations, and W4A4 quantizes both. Weight-only
schemes save memory; the matrix multiply still runs in 16-bit unless
activations are quantized too.

METHODS                                   *quantization-formats-index-methods*

These are the values of `--quantization` other than the
|quantization-formats-index-online-shorthands|. For a pre-quantized
checkpoint vLLM reads `quant_method` from `config.json` and selects the
method, so the flag is normally omitted. A method that does not
correspond to a checkpoint format is used only when it is passed
explicitly.

Names                Implementation                Platforms             Guide
-------------------  ----------------------------  --------------------  -----
awq, awq_marlin,     AutoAWQConfig                 CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide
auto_awq
fp8                  Fp8Config                     CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  --
w4afp8               W4AFP8Config                  CUDA, CPU             --
fbgemm_fp8           FBGEMMFp8Config               CUDA, ROCm, CPU       --
(deprecated)
modelopt             ModelOptFp8Config             CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide
modelopt_fp4         ModelOptNvFp4Config           CUDA, ROCm, CPU       Guide
modelopt_mxfp8,      ModelOptMxFp8Config           CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide
mxfp8
modelopt_int8        ModelOptInt8Config            CUDA, ROCm, CPU       Guide
modelopt_mixed       ModelOptMixedPrecisionConfig  CUDA, ROCm, CPU       Guide
auto_gptq, gptq,     AutoGPTQConfig                CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide
gptq_marlin
compressed-tensors   CompressedTensorsConfig       CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide
experts_int8         ExpertsInt8Config             CUDA, CPU             --
quark                QuarkConfig                   CUDA, ROCm, CPU       Guide
moe_wna16            MoeWNA16Config                CUDA, XPU, CPU        --
torchao              TorchAOConfig                 CUDA, ROCm, CPU       Guide
inc                  INCConfig                     CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide
mxfp4                Mxfp4Config                   CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  --
gpt_oss_mxfp4        GptOssMxfp4Config             CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  --
deepseek_v4_fp8      DeepseekV4FP8Config           CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  --
inkling2             Inkling2QuantConfig           CUDA, CPU             --
humming              HummingConfig                 CUDA, CPU             --
online               OnlineQuantizationConfig      CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  Guide

- Names on one row are aliases of one implementation.
- Platforms lists where vLLM accepts the name at startup. ROCm and XPU
  accept only the names on their allow-list; CUDA and CPU accept every
  name. An accepted method can still have no kernel for a given device;
  see |vllm-quantization-kernels-index|.
- A deprecated method also needs `--allow-deprecated-quantization`.

ONLINE SHORTHANDS               *quantization-formats-index-online-shorthands*

Passed to `--quantization`, a shorthand quantizes the weights of a
checkpoint that is not quantized. The columns show the weight scheme it
applies to linear layers and to MoE expert layers; `–` leaves that kind
of layer unquantized.

Name                Linear weights      MoE weights         Platforms
------------------  ------------------  ------------------  ------------------
fp8_per_tensor      fp8_per_tensor_     fp8_per_tensor_     CUDA, ROCm, XPU,
                    static              static              CPU
fp8_per_block       fp8_per_block_      fp8_per_block_      CUDA, ROCm, XPU,
                    static              static              CPU
fp8_per_channel     fp8_per_channel_    fp8_per_channel_    CUDA, ROCm, XPU,
                    static              static              CPU
mxfp8               mxfp8               mxfp8               CUDA, ROCm, XPU,
                                                            CPU
mxfp4               mxfp4               mxfp4               CUDA, ROCm, XPU,
                                                            CPU
mxfp4_hadamard32    mxfp4 with a        --                   CUDA, ROCm, CPU
                    32-wide Hadamard
                    rotation
int8_per_channel_   --                   int8_per_channel_   CUDA, CPU
weight_only                             static
nvfp4_per_token     --                   FP4 (E2M1), E4M3    CUDA, CPU
                                        per 16 elements,
                                        fp32 per tensor
nvfp4_weight_only   FP4 (E2M1), E4M3    --                   CUDA, CPU
                    per 16 elements,
                    fp32 per tensor
lut_b_moe           --                   lut_b               CUDA, ROCm, CPU

Also a method name: `mxfp8`, `mxfp4`. On a checkpoint that is already
quantized, these select the method of that name instead.

- A shorthand is not a scheme, even when the two share a name: read the
  columns. A cell that is not a name is a scheme that has no name of its
  own and is reachable only through the shorthand.
- Shorthands set weights only. The activation scheme is the default of
  the method that implements the weight scheme, and it can depend on the
  kernel that is selected.
- The same names are accepted inside `--quantization-config`, including
  on a checkpoint that is already quantized. See
  |vllm-quantization-online-index-2|.

SCHEME NAMES                         *quantization-formats-index-scheme-names*

These names are accepted by the `weight` and `activation` fields of
`--quantization-config`:

>bash
    vllm serve <model> --quantization online \
        --quantization-config '{"moe":{"weight":"mxfp4","activation":"mxfp8"}}'
<

Name               Data           Scale          Scale          Online weight
                                                 computed       for
-----------------  -------------  -------------  -------------  --------------
fp8_per_tensor_    FP8 (E4M3)     fp32 per       at load        linear, MoE
static                            tensor
fp8_per_tensor_    FP8 (E4M3)     fp32 per       per forward    --
dynamic                           tensor         pass
fp8_per_token      FP8 (E4M3)     fp32 per       per forward    --
                                  token          pass
fp8_per_channel_   FP8 (E4M3)     fp32 per       at load        linear, MoE
static                            output
                                  channel
fp8_per_block_     FP8 (E4M3)     fp32 per       at load        linear, MoE
static                            128×128 block
fp8_per_block_     FP8 (E4M3)     fp32 per 128   per forward    --
dynamic                           elements       pass
mxfp8              FP8 (E4M3)     E8M0 per 32    at load        linear, MoE
                                  elements       (weight), per
                                                 forward pass
                                                 (activation)
mxfp4              FP4 (E2M1)     E8M0 per 32    at load        linear, MoE
                                  elements       (weight), per
                                                 forward pass
                                                 (activation)
nvfp4_per_token    FP4 (E2M1)     E4M3 per 16    per forward    --
                                  elements,      pass
                                  then fp32 per
                                  token (per
                                  forward pass)
int8_per_channel_  INT8           fp32 per       at load        MoE
static                            output
                                  channel
lut_b              uint8          E4M3 per 8×64  at load        MoE
                                  block
int8_per_group_32  INT8           fp32 per 32    per forward    --
                                  elements       pass
int4_per_group_32  INT4           fp16 per 32    at load        --
                                  elements
int4_per_group_    UINT4,         fp16 per 32    at load        lm_head
32_asym            asymmetric     elements

- Data is the element format. ExMy is a float with x exponent and y
  mantissa bits: E4M3 is 8-bit and E2M1 is 4-bit, each with a sign bit.
  E8M0 is an unsigned 8-bit exponent, i.e. a power of two.
- Scale is the format of the scale factor and what shares one value. A
  second entry is an outer scale applied on top of the first.
- Scale computed separates static scales, which are stored in the
  checkpoint or computed once at load, from dynamic ones, which are
  computed from the tensor on every forward pass. Weights use static
  scales. Dynamic scales are how activations are quantized without
  calibration data.
- Online weight for lists the kinds of layer that online quantization
  can quantize to this scheme. `lm_head` marks a scheme that applies
  only to the lm_head, selected with `targets`. Every name parses on
  both fields, but a name with `–` here is rejected or ignored as an
  online weight, and only some methods accept an `activation` override.
  See |vllm-quantization-online-index-2|.

The table shows each scheme as vLLM's
|vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.utils.quant_utils.QuantKey|
describes it. Formats that are not a data format plus scales are
approximated: `lut_b` stores 3-bit indices into a codebook, which
appears here as the scale, and an E8M0 scale per 32 elements that the
key does not describe.

MXFP8 and MXFP4 are the OCP Microscaling
https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-microscaling-formats-mx-v1-0-spec-final-pdf
formats. With `b` bits per element and an `s`-bit scale shared by `g`
elements, a weight costs about `b + s / g` bits per parameter: 4.25 for
MXFP4 and 4.5 for NVFP4.

SEE ALSO                                 *quantization-formats-index-see-also*

- |vllm-quantization-kernels-index|: which kernel runs a layer, and how
  to pin one.
- |vllm-quantization-online-index-2|: the `--quantization-config`
  schema, per-layer targeting, and re-quantizing a quantized checkpoint.
- |vllm-quantization-quantized_kvcache-index|: a separate setting from
  weight quantization.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
FP8 ViT Encoder Attention               *vllm-quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index*

For visual understanding workloads with large images (e.g. QHD, 4K) and
relatively short text prompts/generation, the ViT encoder attention can
become a significant bottleneck, especially when the text model is
quantized (e.g. NVFP4). vLLM supports optional FP8 quantization for ViT
encoder attention via FlashInfer cuDNN on NVIDIA GPUs and AITER on AMD
GPUs. Q/K/V are quantized on-the-fly to FP8 before the attention call.

  Note: ~

  - Currently supports Qwen3-VL family models only (`qwen3_vl`,
    `qwen3_vl_moe`, `qwen3_5`, `qwen3_5_moe`, and other models using
    Qwen3 ViT).
  - Dynamic scaling is not compatible with ViT full CUDA graphs.
  - Performance gains are mostly visible at QHD/4K resolutions or
    multi-image requests. Smaller images may see no speedup due to
    quantization overhead (3 quantization kernel launches + un-padding).
  - FP8 tensor-core speedup is more pronounced on GB300 than GB200.
  - On ROCm, packed variable-length image and video batches are
    supported by AITER's native varlen FP8 attention.

REQUIREMENTS                    *quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index-requirements*

- NVIDIA: FlashInfer cuDNN backend with cuDNN >= 9.17.1.
- AMD: AITER with `flash_attn_varlen_fp8_pertensor_func` support on
  gfx942 (MI300 series) or gfx950 (MI350 series).

USAGE                                  *quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index-usage*

Enable FP8 ViT attention by passing `--mm-encoder-attn-dtype fp8` and
selecting the backend for the current platform:

>bash
    vllm serve $MODEL \
        --mm-encoder-attn-backend FLASHINFER \
        --mm-encoder-attn-dtype fp8

    # AMD ROCm
    vllm serve $MODEL \
        --mm-encoder-attn-backend ROCM_AITER_FA \
        --mm-encoder-attn-dtype fp8
<
By default (no scale file), dynamic scaling is used: a 16-entry circular
buffer of observed Q/K/V amax values drives per-forward scale updates.
This matches BF16 accuracy without any calibration but adds a small
per-forward overhead.

   *quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index-calibrate-once-reuse-workflow-recommended*
CALIBRATE-ONCE, REUSE WORKFLOW (RECOMMENDED)

For production, calibrate static scales on a representative dataset once
and reuse them to avoid the dynamic overhead:

>bash
    # Step 1: calibrate and save scales (runs dynamic scaling for 16 passes,
    # then dumps the learned scales to JSON).
    vllm bench mm-processor \
        --model $MODEL --mm-encoder-attn-backend $MM_ATTN_BACKEND \
        --mm-encoder-attn-dtype fp8 \
        --mm-encoder-fp8-scale-save-path /path/to/scales.json \
        --dataset-name hf --dataset-path lmarena-ai/VisionArena-Chat \
        --num-prompts 100

    # Step 2: serve with static scales (no dynamic overhead).
    vllm serve $MODEL \
        --mm-encoder-attn-backend $MM_ATTN_BACKEND \
        --mm-encoder-attn-dtype fp8 \
        --mm-encoder-fp8-scale-path /path/to/scales.json
<
Saved scales are multiplied by `--mm-encoder-fp8-scale-save-margin`
(default `1.5`) to leave headroom against activation outliers not
present in the calibration set. The default has been validated to
generalize across datasets (e.g. VisionArena-Chat calibration maintains
BF16 accuracy on ChartQA).

SCALE FILE FORMAT          *quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index-scale-file-format*

>json
    {
        "visual.blocks.0.attn.attn": {"q": 224.0, "k": 198.0, "v": 210.0},
        "visual.blocks.1.attn.attn": {"q": 218.0, "k": 195.0, "v": 207.0}
    }
<
Keys `q_scale` / `k_scale` / `v_scale` are accepted as aliases.

PERFORMANCE                      *quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index-performance*

Core cuDNN attention kernel (PyTorch profiler,
`cudnn_generated_fort_native_sdpa_sm100_flash_fprop`, head_dim=128,
seq_len=8192):

Hardware                BF16              FP8               Speedup
----------------------  ----------------  ----------------  ------------------
GB200                   350 us            312 us            1.12x
GB300                   300 us            211 us            1.42x

End-to-end encoder forward time (Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B-Instruct on GB200, 3
images/request):

Resolution                                    BF16 median  FP8 median  Speedup
--------------------------------------------  -----------  ----------  -------
HD (720x1280)                                 31.77 ms     36.39 ms    0.87x
FullHD (1080x1920)                            57.99 ms     58.73 ms    ~same
QHD (1440x2560)                               131.83 ms    122.30 ms   1.08x
4K (2160x3840)                                543.44 ms    460.31 ms   1.18x

Crossover is around FullHD with 3 images/request. At QHD and above, FP8
wins.

Complete AITER attention call on MI300X (BF16 input, 16 heads,
head_dim=72; FP8 includes Q/K/V quantization):

Sequence length                                 AITER BF16  AITER FP8  Speedup
----------------------------------------------  ----------  ---------  -------
2304                                            0.467 ms    0.337 ms   1.38x
4096                                            0.812 ms    0.764 ms   1.06x
8192                                            2.555 ms    2.364 ms   1.08x
16384                                           9.769 ms    8.655 ms   1.13x

ACCURACY                            *quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index-accuracy*

ChartQA, Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, 500 samples. FP8 static uses scales
calibrated on VisionArena-Chat (with default 1.5x margin):

Metric                                          BF16   FP8 dynamic  FP8 static
----------------------------------------------  -----  -----------  ----------
relaxed_accuracy                                0.780  0.776        0.780
anywhere_accuracy                               0.806  0.816        0.814
exact_match                                     0.584  0.582        0.578

All three configurations match within statistical noise, confirming that
static scales calibrated on one dataset generalize to another.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
GGUF                                            *vllm-quantization-gguf-index*

  Warning: ~

  Please note that GGUF support in vLLM is highly experimental and
  under-optimized at the moment, it might be incompatible with other
  features. Currently, you can use GGUF as a way to reduce memory
  footprint. If you encounter any issues, please report them to the vLLM
  team.

  Note: ~

  GGUF support has migrated to OOT vllm-gguf-plugin
  https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-gguf-plugin. Make sure you have
  GGUF plugin installed before serving a GGUF model.

Before serving a GGUF model, make sure to install the vllm-gguf-plugin
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-gguf-plugin:

>bash
    uv pip install vllm-gguf-plugin
<
To run a GGUF model with vLLM, you can use the `repo_id:quant_type`
format to load directly from HuggingFace. For example, to load a Q4_K_M
quantized model from unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF
https://huggingface.co/unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF:

>bash
    # We recommend using the tokenizer from base model to avoid long-time and buggy tokenizer conversion.
    vllm serve unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF:Q4_K_M --tokenizer Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B
<
You can also add `--tensor-parallel-size 2` to enable tensor parallelism
inference with 2 GPUs:

>bash
    vllm serve unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF:Q4_K_M \
       --tokenizer Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B \
       --tensor-parallel-size 2
<
Alternatively, you can download and use a local GGUF file:

>bash
    wget https://huggingface.co/unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF/resolve/main/Qwen3-0.6B-Q4_K_M.gguf
    vllm serve ./Qwen3-0.6B-Q4_K_M.gguf --tokenizer Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B
<
  Warning: ~

  We recommend using the tokenizer from base model instead of GGUF
  model. Because the tokenizer conversion from GGUF is time-consuming
  and unstable, especially for some models with large vocab size.

GGUF assumes that HuggingFace can convert the metadata to a config file.
In case HuggingFace doesn't support your model you can manually create a
config and pass it as hf-config-path

>bash
    # If your model is not supported by HuggingFace you can manually provide a HuggingFace compatible config path
    vllm serve unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF:Q4_K_M \
       --tokenizer Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B \
       --hf-config-path Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B
<
You can also use the GGUF model directly through the LLM entrypoint:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

      # In this script, we demonstrate how to pass input to the chat method:
      conversation = [
         {
            "role": "system",
            "content": "You are a helpful assistant",
         },
         {
            "role": "user",
            "content": "Hello",
         },
         {
            "role": "assistant",
            "content": "Hello! How can I assist you today?",
         },
         {
            "role": "user",
            "content": "Write an essay about the importance of higher education.",
         },
      ]

      # Create a sampling params object.
      sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.8, top_p=0.95)

      # Create an LLM using repo_id:quant_type format.
      llm = LLM(
         model="unsloth/Qwen3-0.6B-GGUF:Q4_K_M",
         tokenizer="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B",
      )
      # Generate texts from the prompts. The output is a list of RequestOutput objects
      # that contain the prompt, generated text, and other information.
      outputs = llm.chat(conversation, sampling_params)

      # Print the outputs.
      for output in outputs:
         prompt = output.prompt
         generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
         print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}, Generated text: {generated_text!r}")
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
GPTQModel                                  *vllm-quantization-gptqmodel-index*

To create a new 4-bit or 8-bit GPTQ quantized model, you can leverage
GPTQModel https://github.com/ModelCloud/GPTQModel from ModelCloud.AI.

Quantization reduces the model's precision from BF16/FP16 (16-bits) to
INT4 (4-bits) or INT8 (8-bits) which significantly reduces the total
model memory footprint while at-the-same-time increasing inference
performance.

Compatible GPTQModel quantized models can leverage the `Marlin` and
`Machete` vLLM custom kernels to maximize batching
transactions-per-second `tps` and token-latency performance for both
Ampere (A100+) and Hopper (H100+) Nvidia GPUs. These two kernels are
highly optimized by vLLM and NeuralMagic (now part of Redhat) to allow
world-class inference performance of quantized GPTQ models.

GPTQModel is one of the few quantization toolkits in the world that
allows `Dynamic` per-module quantization where different layers and/or
modules within a llm model can be further optimized with custom
quantization parameters. `Dynamic` quantization is fully integrated into
vLLM and backed up by support from the ModelCloud.AI team. Please refer
to GPTQModel readme
https://github.com/ModelCloud/GPTQModel?tab=readme-ov-file#dynamic-quantization-per-module-quantizeconfig-override
for more details on this and other advanced features.

INSTALLATION                       *quantization-gptqmodel-index-installation*

You can quantize your own models by installing GPTQModel
https://github.com/ModelCloud/GPTQModel or picking one of the
5000+ models on Huggingface https://huggingface.co/models?search=gptq.

>bash
    pip install -U gptqmodel --no-build-isolation -v
<

QUANTIZING A MODEL           *quantization-gptqmodel-index-quantizing-a-model*

After installing GPTQModel, you are ready to quantize a model. Please
refer to the GPTQModel readme
https://github.com/ModelCloud/GPTQModel/?tab=readme-ov-file#quantization
for further details.

Here is an example of how to quantize
`meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct`:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from datasets import load_dataset
      from gptqmodel import GPTQModel, QuantizeConfig

      model_id = "meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct"
      quant_path = "Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct-gptqmodel-4bit"

      calibration_dataset = load_dataset(
          "allenai/c4",
          data_files="en/c4-train.00001-of-01024.json.gz",
          split="train",
      ).select(range(1024))["text"]

      quant_config = QuantizeConfig(bits=4, group_size=128)

      model = GPTQModel.load(model_id, quant_config)

      # increase `batch_size` to match gpu/vram specs to speed up quantization
      model.quantize(calibration_dataset, batch_size=2)

      model.save(quant_path)
<

            *quantization-gptqmodel-index-running-a-quantized-model-with-vllm*
RUNNING A QUANTIZED MODEL WITH VLLM

To run a GPTQModel quantized model with vLLM, you can use
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B-gptqmodel-4bit-vortex-v2
https://huggingface.co/ModelCloud/DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B-gptqmodel-4bit-vortex-v2
with the following command:

>bash
    python examples/deployment/llm_engine_example.py \
        --model ModelCloud/DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B-gptqmodel-4bit-vortex-v2
<

          *quantization-gptqmodel-index-using-gptqmodel-with-vllms-python-api*
USING GPTQMODEL WITH VLLM'S PYTHON API

GPTQModel quantized models are also supported directly through the LLM
entrypoint:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

      # Sample prompts.
      prompts = [
          "Hello, my name is",
          "The president of the United States is",
          "The capital of France is",
          "The future of AI is",
      ]

      # Create a sampling params object.
      sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.6, top_p=0.9)

      # Create an LLM.
      llm = LLM(model="ModelCloud/DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B-gptqmodel-4bit-vortex-v2")

      # Generate texts from the prompts. The output is a list of RequestOutput objects
      # that contain the prompt, generated text, and other information.
      outputs = llm.generate(prompts, sampling_params)

      # Print the outputs.
      print("-"*50)
      for output in outputs:
          prompt = output.prompt
          generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
          print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}\nGenerated text: {generated_text!r}")
          print("-"*50)
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Intel Quantization Support                     *vllm-quantization-inc-index-3*

AutoRound https://github.com/intel/auto-round is Intel's advanced
quantization algorithm designed for large language models(LLMs). It
produces highly efficient INT2, INT3, INT4, INT8, MXFP8, MXFP4, NVFP4,
and GGUF quantized models, balancing accuracy and inference performance.
AutoRound is also part of the Intel® Neural Compressor
https://github.com/intel/neural-compressor. For a deeper introduction,
see the AutoRound step-by-step guide
https://github.com/intel/auto-round/blob/main/docs/step_by_step.md.

KEY FEATURES                           *quantization-inc-index-3-key-features*

✅ Superior Accuracy Delivers strong performance even at 2--3 bits
example models https://huggingface.co/collections/OPEA/2-3-bits

✅ Fast Mixed `Bits`/`Dtypes` Scheme Generation Automatically configure
in minutes

✅ Support for exporting AutoRound, AutoAWQ, AutoGPTQ, and GGUF formats

✅ 10+ vision-language models (VLMs) are supported

✅ Per-layer mixed-bit quantization for fine-grained control

✅ RTN (Round-To-Nearest) mode for quick quantization with slight
accuracy loss

✅ Multiple quantization recipes: best, base, and light

✅ Advanced utilities such as immediate packing and support for 10+
backends

               *quantization-inc-index-3-supported-recipes-on-intel-platforms*
SUPPORTED RECIPES ON INTEL PLATFORMS

On Intel platforms, AutoRound recipes are being enabled progressively by
format and hardware. Currently, vLLM supports:

- `W4A16`: weight-only, 4-bit weights with 16-bit activations
- `W8A16`: weight-only, 8-bit weights with 16-bit activations

Additional recipes and formats will be supported in future releases.

QUANTIZING A MODEL               *quantization-inc-index-3-quantizing-a-model*

Installation                           *quantization-inc-index-3-installation*

>bash
    uv pip install auto-round
<

Quantize with CLI                 *quantization-inc-index-3-quantize-with-cli*

>bash
    auto-round \
        --model Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B \
        --scheme W4A16 \
        --format auto_round \
        --output_dir ./tmp_autoround
<

Quantize with Python API   *quantization-inc-index-3-quantize-with-python-api*

>python
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer
    from auto_round import AutoRound

    model_name = "Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B"
    autoround = AutoRound(model_name, scheme="W4A16")

    # the best accuracy, 4-5X slower, low_gpu_mem_usage could save ~20G but ~30% slower
    # autoround = AutoRound(model, tokenizer, nsamples=512, iters=1000, low_gpu_mem_usage=True, bits=bits, group_size=group_size, sym=sym)

    # 2-3X speedup, slight accuracy drop at W4G128
    # autoround = AutoRound(model, tokenizer, nsamples=128, iters=50, lr=5e-3, bits=bits, group_size=group_size, sym=sym )

    output_dir = "./tmp_autoround"
    # format= 'auto_round'(default), 'auto_gptq', 'auto_awq'
    autoround.quantize_and_save(output_dir, format="auto_round")
<

      --tasks gsm8k \
