----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Quantization                                *vllm-features-quantization-index*

Quantization trades off model precision for smaller memory footprint,
allowing large models to be run on a wider range of devices.

  Tip: ~

  To get started with quantization, see
  |vllm-quantization-llm_compressor-index|, a library for optimizing
  models for deployment with vLLM that supports FP8, INT8, INT4, and
  other quantization formats.

  For the names vLLM accepts for methods and schemes, see
  |vllm-quantization-formats-index|. For the kernel backends of
  quantized linear layers, see |vllm-quantization-kernels-index|.

The following are the supported quantization formats for vLLM:

- |vllm-quantization-auto_awq-index-2|
- |vllm-quantization-bnb-index|
- |vllm-quantization-gptqmodel-index|
- |vllm-quantization-inc-index-3|
- |vllm-quantization-llm_compressor-index|
  - |vllm-llm_compressor-fp8-index|
  - |vllm-llm_compressor-int4-index|
  - |vllm-llm_compressor-int8_w4a8-index|
  - |vllm-llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index|
- |vllm-quantization-modelopt-index-2|
- |vllm-quantization-online-index-2|
- |vllm-quantization-quark-index-2|
- |vllm-quantization-quantized_kvcache-index|
- |vllm-quantization-torchao-index-2|
- |vllm-quantization-fp8_vit_attn-index|

                       *features-quantization-index-selecting-linear-backends*
SELECTING LINEAR BACKENDS

To choose the kernel backend of quantized linear layers, for all of them
with `--linear-backend` or per quantization type with
`linear_backend_per_quant`, see
|quantization-kernels-index-selecting-a-backend|.

                  *features-quantization-index-hadamard-input-transforms-quip*
HADAMARD INPUT TRANSFORMS (QUIP)

Compressed-tensors NVFP4 checkpoints with a QuIP-style Hadamard input
transform (`transform_config` with `type: hadamard` and `head_dim` of
16, 32, 64, or 128) run on the Humming backend by default on compute
capability 10.0 and newer. The Hadamard is fused with input
quantization, and the activation dtype declared by the checkpoint is
used, with no fallback to 16-bit activations.

Other transforms, older GPUs, and an explicit `--linear-backend` other
than `auto` or `humming` use the generic transform path, which applies
the transform separately before the selected kernel.

  Note: ~

  The `fp_quant` quantization method and the QuTLASS backend have been
  removed. Use a compressed-tensors checkpoint instead.

  Note: ~

  Marlin kernels no longer quantize activations to INT8 or FP8, and the
  `VLLM_MARLIN_INPUT_DTYPE` environment variable has been removed
  (setting it is an error). Marlin always runs with 16-bit activations.
  To run GPTQ, AWQ, or compressed-tensors weight-only checkpoints with
  8-bit activations (W4A8), use the `humming` quantization method and
  set the activation dtype with `VLLM_HUMMING_INPUT_QUANT_CONFIG`, for
  example:

  >python
      VLLM_HUMMING_INPUT_QUANT_CONFIG='{"dtype":"int8"}' \
        vllm serve <model> --quantization humming
<
  Use `{"dtype":"float8e4m3"}` for FP8 activations. Checkpoints that
  declare 8-bit activations, such as compressed-tensors W4A8, select a
  kernel that supports them automatically.

               *features-quantization-index-activation-quantization-fallbacks*
ACTIVATION QUANTIZATION FALLBACKS

A quantized checkpoint (or `--quantization` / `--quantization-config`)
requests both a weight and an activation scheme, for example FP8 W8A8 or
NVFP4 W4A4. When the kernels that run the requested activation
quantization are unavailable for the device or deployment, vLLM by
default falls back to a weight-only kernel (Marlin, Humming, the CPU
W8A16 kernels) that keeps the quantized weights but runs activations in
16-bit, so W8A8 runs as W8A16 and W4A4 as W4A16. This costs no accuracy,
but it is not the scheme the checkpoint was calibrated and benchmarked
with, and it is logged once as a warning:

>text
    MarlinExperts runs QuantKey(...) activations as unquantized. Set VLLM_STRICT_QUANT_SCHEME=1 to forbid this fallback.
<
Set `VLLM_STRICT_QUANT_SCHEME=1` (default `0`) to forbid these
fallbacks. Kernel selection then skips kernels that would not run the
requested activation quantization and continues down the priority list,
so it can still land on a slower kernel that does (for example NVFP4
emulation). If no kernel is left, or an explicitly requested
`--moe-backend` / `--linear-backend` cannot run the scheme, startup
fails with the reason. Humming input-schema fallbacks are forbidden the
same way.

Strict mode only covers dropping or changing a requested activation
quantization at kernel selection. It does not cover backends that add
activation quantization to a weight-only request, or per-shape and
per-batch-size fallbacks inside a selected kernel.

SUPPORTED HARDWARE            *features-quantization-index-supported-hardware*

The table below shows the compatibility of various quantization
implementations with different hardware platforms in vLLM:

Implementation      Volta  Turing  Ampere  Ada  Hopper  AMD  Intel  x86   Arm
                                                        GPU  GPU    CPU   CPU
------------------  -----  ------  ------  ---  ------  ---  -----  ----  ----
AWQ                 ❌     ✅︎     ✅︎     ✅︎  ✅︎     ❌   ✅︎    ✅︎   ❌
GPTQ                ✅︎    ✅︎     ✅︎     ✅︎  ✅︎     ❌   ✅︎    ✅︎   ❌
Marlin              ❌     ✅︎*    ✅︎     ✅︎  ✅︎     ❌   ❌     ❌    ❌
(GPTQ/AWQ/FP8/FP4)
llm-compressor      ❌     ✅︎     ✅︎     ✅︎  ✅︎     ❌   ❌     ✅︎   ✅︎

llm-compressor      ❌     ❌      ❌      ❌   ❌      ❌   ❌     ❌    ✅︎

llm-compressor FP8  ❌     ❌      ❌      ✅︎  ✅︎     ✅︎  ❌     ❌    ❌
(W8A8)

- Volta refers to SM 7.0, Turing to SM 7.5, Ampere to SM 8.0/8.6, Ada to
  SM 8.9, and Hopper to SM 9.0.
- ✅︎ indicates that the quantization method is supported on the
  specified hardware.
- ❌ indicates that the quantization method is not supported on the
  specified hardware.
- All Intel Gaudi quantization support has been migrated to vLLM-Gaudi
  https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-gaudi.
- *Turing does not support Marlin MXFP4.

  Note: ~

  For information on quantization support on Google TPU, please refer to
  the TPU-Inference Feature Matrix
  https://docs.vllm.ai/projects/tpu/en/latest/features/#quantization-support
  documentation.

  Note: ~

  This compatibility chart is subject to change as vLLM continues to
  evolve and expand its support for different hardware platforms and
  quantization methods.

  For the most up-to-date information on hardware support and
  quantization methods, please refer to
  vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization
  https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/tree/main/vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization
  or consult with the vLLM development team.

                *features-quantization-index-out-of-tree-quantization-plugins*
OUT-OF-TREE QUANTIZATION PLUGINS

vLLM supports registering custom, out-of-tree quantization methods using
the `@register_quantization_config` decorator. This allows you to
implement and use your own quantization schemes without modifying the
vLLM codebase.

        *features-quantization-index-registering-a-custom-quantization-method*
Registering a Custom Quantization Method

To register a custom quantization method, create a class that inherits
from
|vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.base_config.QuantizationConfig|
and decorate it with `@register_quantization_config`. The
`get_quant_method` dispatches to the appropriate quantize method based
on the layer type:

>python
    import torch
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization import (
        register_quantization_config,
    )
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.base_config import (
        QuantizationConfig,
        QuantizeMethodBase,
    )
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.linear import LinearBase
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.fused_moe import FusedMoE

    @register_quantization_config("my_quant")
    class MyQuantConfig(QuantizationConfig):
        """Custom quantization config."""

        def get_name(self) -> str:
            return "my_quant"

        def get_supported_act_dtypes(self) -> list:
            return [torch.float16, torch.bfloat16]

        @classmethod
        def get_min_capability(cls) -> int:
            # Minimum GPU compute capability, -1 for no restriction
            return -1

        @staticmethod
        def get_config_filenames() -> list[str]:
            # Config files to search for in model directory
            return []

        @classmethod
        def from_config(cls, config: dict) -> "MyQuantConfig":
            # Create config from model's quantization config
            return cls()

        def get_quant_method(
            self, layer: torch.nn.Module, prefix: str
        ) -> QuantizeMethodBase | None:
            # Dispatch based on layer type
            # NOTE: you only need to implement methods you care about
            if isinstance(layer, LinearBase):
                return MyQuantLinearMethod()
            elif isinstance(layer, FusedMoE):
                return MyQuantMoEMethod(layer.moe_config)
            return None
<

             *features-quantization-index-required-quantizationconfig-methods*
Required QuantizationConfig Methods

Your custom
|vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.base_config.QuantizationConfig|
subclass must implement these abstract methods:

Method                           Description
-------------------------------  ---------------------------------------------
get_name()                       Returns the name of the quantization method
    get_supported_act_dtypes()       Returns list of supported activation dtypes
                                 (e.g., torch.float16)
get_min_capability()             Returns minimum GPU compute capability (e.g.,
                                 80 for Ampere, -1 for no restriction)
get_config_filenames()           Returns list of config filenames to search
                                 for in model directory
from_config(config)              Class method to create config from model's
                                 quantization config dict
get_quant_method(layer, prefix)  Returns the quantization method for a given
                                 layer, or None to skip

          *features-quantization-index-implementing-a-quantized-linear-method*
Implementing a Quantized Linear Method

For linear layers, return a
|vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.base_config.QuantizeMethodBase|
subclass from `get_quant_method`. You can extend
|vllm.model_executor.layers.linear.UnquantizedLinearMethod| as a
starting point:

>python
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.linear import UnquantizedLinearMethod

    class MyQuantLinearMethod(UnquantizedLinearMethod):
        """Custom quantization method for linear layers."""

        def create_weights(
            self, layer: torch.nn.Module, *weight_args, **extra_weight_attrs
        ):
            # Create quantized weights for the layer
            ...

        def apply(
            self,
            layer: torch.nn.Module,
            x: torch.Tensor,
            bias: torch.Tensor | None = None,
        ) -> torch.Tensor:
            # Apply custom quantization logic here
            ...
<

             *features-quantization-index-implementing-a-quantized-moe-method*
Implementing a Quantized MoE Method

For Mixture of Experts (MoE) models, return a `FusedMoEMethodBase`
subclass from `get_quant_method`. You can use
|vllm.model_executor.layers.fused_moe.unquantized_fused_moe_method.UnquantizedFusedMoEMethod|
to skip MoE quantization:

>python
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.fused_moe.layer import UnquantizedFusedMoEMethod
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.fused_moe.fused_moe_method_base import (
        FusedMoEMethodBase,
    )
    from vllm.model_executor.layers.fused_moe.config import FusedMoEQuantConfig

    class MyQuantMoEMethod(FusedMoEMethodBase):
        """Custom quantization method for MoE layers."""

        def create_weights(
            self,
            layer: torch.nn.Module,
            num_experts: int,
            hidden_size: int,
            intermediate_size_per_partition: int,
            params_dtype: torch.dtype,
            **extra_weight_attrs,
        ):
            # Create quantized weights for the MoE layer
            ...

        def apply(
            self,
            layer: torch.nn.Module,
            router: "FusedMoERouter",
            x: torch.Tensor,
            router_logits: torch.Tensor,
        ) -> torch.Tensor:
            # Apply MoE computation with quantized weights
            ...

        def get_fused_moe_quant_config(
            self, layer: torch.nn.Module
        ) -> FusedMoEQuantConfig | None:
            # Return the MoE quantization configuration
            ...
<
See existing implementations like
|vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.fp8.Fp8MoEMethod| in
`vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization/fp8.py` for reference.

Using the Plugin                *features-quantization-index-using-the-plugin*

Once registered, you can use your custom quantization method with vLLM:

>python
    # Register your quantization method (import the module containing your config)
    import my_quant_plugin

    from vllm import LLM

    # Use the custom quantization method
    llm = LLM(model="your-model", quantization="my_quant")
<
For more information on the plugin system, see the
|vllm-design-plugin_system-index|.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Kernel Backends                              *vllm-quantization-kernels-index*

A quantized layer runs on a kernel chosen at load time from its weight
and activation scheme, the platform, and the device. This page covers
quantized linear layers: the backends that provide kernels and how to
select one. MoE expert layers are selected separately with
`--moe-backend`; see Fused MoE Kernel Features
https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/design/moe_kernel_features/.

The tables are generated at docs build time by
`docs/mkdocs/gen_files/generate_quantization_tables.py` from the kernel
registries and `_LINEAR_BACKEND_KERNEL_MAP` in
`vllm/model_executor/kernels/linear/__init__.py`. A kernel added to a
registry appears here on the next build.

LINEAR BACKENDS                   *quantization-kernels-index-linear-backends*

These are the values of `--linear-backend`, with the platforms and
quantization types each has a kernel for:

Backend             Platforms             Quantization types
------------------  --------------------  ------------------------------------
b12x                CUDA                  FP8, FP8 block, MXFP8, NVFP4, MXFP4
cutlass             CUDA                  INT8, FP8, FP8 block, WNA16, NVFP4
flashinfer_cutlass  CUDA                  FP8, FP8 block, MXFP8, NVFP4, MXFP4
flashinfer_cutedsl  CUDA                  MXFP8, NVFP4
flashinfer_trtllm   CUDA                  MXFP8, NVFP4
flashinfer_cudnn    CUDA                  NVFP4
flashinfer_b12x     CUDA                  NVFP4
humming             CUDA                  INT8, FP8, FP8 block, FP8 W8A16,
                                          WNA16, MXFP8, NVFP4, MXFP6, MXFP4
marlin              CUDA                  FP8, FP8 block, FP8 W8A16, WNA16,
                                          MXFP8, NVFP4, MXFP4
triton              CUDA, ROCm, XPU       INT8, FP8 block, WNA16
deep_gemm           CUDA                  FP8 block
torch               CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU  FP8, FP8 block, MXFP8, NVFP4, MXFP4
aiter               ROCm                  INT8, FP8, FP8 block, MXFP4
machete             CUDA                  WNA16
fbgemm              CUDA                  NVFP4
conch               CUDA, ROCm            WNA16
exllama             CUDA, ROCm            WNA16
emulation           CUDA, ROCm, XPU       MXFP8, NVFP4, MXFP6, MXFP4
xpu                 XPU                   FP8, FP8 block
xpu_woq             XPU                   FP8, FP8 W8A16

- A quantization type is named for the weight format. FP8 block is FP8
  with block-wise scales, FP8 W8A16 is FP8 weights with 16-bit
  activations, and WNA16 is integer weights such as GPTQ, AWQ, and
  compressed-tensors W4A16 and W4A8.
- A backend listed for a platform and type can still be unavailable on a
  device: a kernel needs its library installed and can require a GPU
  generation, a layer shape, or a scale layout.

HOW A BACKEND IS CHOSEN   *quantization-kernels-index-how-a-backend-is-chosen*

By default (`--linear-backend auto`) vLLM tries the kernels registered
for the platform and quantization type in priority order and uses the
first one that supports the device and can implement the layer. The
selected kernel class is logged at startup for most quantization types,
and that log is the authoritative answer for a deployment.

A layer that requests quantized activations can be given a kernel that
runs 16-bit activations when no other kernel is available.
`VLLM_STRICT_QUANT_SCHEME=1` forbids this; see
|features-quantization-index-activation-quantization-fallbacks|.

SELECTING A BACKEND           *quantization-kernels-index-selecting-a-backend*

`--linear-backend` sets the backend for every quantization type:

>bash
    vllm serve <model> --linear-backend marlin
<
`linear_backend_per_quant` in `--kernel-config` sets the backend for one
quantization type and takes precedence over `--linear-backend`. Other
types keep their setting:

>bash
    vllm serve <model> \
      --kernel-config '{"linear_backend_per_quant":{"nvfp4_w4a16":"humming"}}'
<
Its keys are:

Quantization type    linear_backend_per_quant key       Platforms
-------------------  ---------------------------------  ----------------------
INT8                 int8_w8a8                          CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU
FP8                  fp8_w8a8                           CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU
FP8 block            fp8_block_w8a8                     CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU
FP8 W8A16            w8a16_fp8                          CUDA, XPU
WNA16                mixed_precision                    CUDA, ROCm, XPU, CPU
MXFP8                mxfp8                              CUDA, ROCm, XPU
NVFP4                nvfp4_w4a16, nvfp4_w4a4            CUDA, ROCm
MXFP6                mxfp6                              CUDA, ROCm
MXFP4                mxfp4                              CUDA, ROCm, XPU

`w8a8`, `w8a16`, `w4a4`, and `w4a16` in a key give the weight and
activation bits the layer requests. NVFP4 weight-only layers
(`nvfp4_w4a16`) use only the kernels that run 16-bit activations, so a
backend listed for NVFP4 can have no kernel for that key.

With a backend set, a quantization type is restricted to that backend's
kernels:

- If the backend has no kernel for the type on the platform, the type
  keeps automatic selection and a warning is logged.
- If the backend has a kernel but it cannot run on the device or
  implement the layer, startup fails.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
FP8 W8A8                                       *vllm-llm_compressor-fp8-index*

vLLM supports FP8 (8-bit floating point) weight and activation
quantization using hardware acceleration on GPUs such as Nvidia H100 and
AMD MI300x. Ada Lovelace, Hopper, and Blackwell GPUs are supported for
W8A8. Turing/Ampere GPUs are supported for W8A16 (weight-only FP8)
utilizing Marlin kernels. Quantization of models with FP8 allows for a
2x reduction in model memory requirements and up to a 1.6x improvement
in throughput with minimal impact on accuracy.

Please visit the HF collection of
quantized FP8 checkpoints of popular LLMs ready to use with vLLM
https://huggingface.co/collections/neuralmagic/fp8-llms-for-vllm-666742ed2b78b7ac8df13127.

The FP8 types typically supported in hardware have two distinct
representations, each useful in different scenarios:

- E4M3: Consists of 1 sign bit, 4 exponent bits, and 3 bits of mantissa.
  It can store values up to +/-448 and `nan`.
- E5M2: Consists of 1 sign bit, 5 exponent bits, and 2 bits of mantissa.
  It can store values up to +/-57344, +/- `inf`, and `nan`. The tradeoff
  for the increased dynamic range is lower precision of the stored
  values.

  Note: ~

  FP8 computation is supported on NVIDIA GPUs with compute capability >=
  8.9 (Ada Lovelace, Hopper, Blackwell). FP8 models will run on compute
  capability >= 7.5 (Turing) as weight-only W8A16, utilizing FP8 Marlin.

  GEMM kernel selection: ~

  vLLM picks an FP8 GEMM kernel automatically at load time and lists it
  in the
  |usage-troubleshooting-index-check-which-backends-a-model-runs-on|
  logged at startup. For block-quantized checkpoints on CUDA it tries,
  in order: a FlashInfer/DeepGEMM hybrid (Hopper only), DeepGEMM,
  CUTLASS, Marlin, Triton, Humming, then a PyTorch fallback. GPUs
  without native FP8 support (e.g. Turing/Ampere) land on weight-only
  (W8A16) Marlin.

  If inference hangs with no error, try `VLLM_USE_DEEP_GEMM=0` or
  `--linear-backend cutlass`.

  `--linear-backend` only affects quantized linear layers; MoE experts
  use the separate `--moe-backend`. An explicit backend that is not
  supported on your hardware raises an error rather than falling back.
  The full list is documented under |cli-serve-index-kernelconfig| in
  the CLI reference and shown by `vllm serve --help=KernelConfig`.

INSTALLATION                           *llm_compressor-fp8-index-installation*

To produce performant FP8 quantized models with vLLM, you'll need to
install the llm-compressor
https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/ library:

>bash
    (venv-llm-compressor) pip install llmcompressor
<
Additionally, install `vllm` and `lm-evaluation-harness` for evaluation:

>bash
    (venv-vllm) pip install vllm "lm-eval[api]>=0.4.12"
<
Please use separate environments for vLLM and llm-compressor as they
might not work together.

QUANTIZATION PROCESS           *llm_compressor-fp8-index-quantization-process*

The quantization process involves three main steps:

1. Loading the model
2. Applying quantization
3. Evaluating accuracy in vLLM
1. Loading the Model            *llm_compressor-fp8-index-1-loading-the-model*

Load your model and tokenizer using the standard `transformers`
AutoModel classes:

>python
    from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM

    MODEL_ID = "meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct"
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        MODEL_ID,
        device_map="auto",
        dtype="auto",
    )
    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID)
<

2. Applying Quantization    *llm_compressor-fp8-index-2-applying-quantization*

For FP8 quantization, we can recover accuracy with simple RTN
quantization. We recommend targeting all `Linear` layers using the
`FP8_DYNAMIC` scheme, which uses:

- Static, per-channel quantization on the weights
- Dynamic, per-token quantization on the activations

Since simple RTN does not require data for weight quantization and the
activations are quantized dynamically, we do not need any calibration
data for this quantization flow.

>python
    from llmcompressor import oneshot
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.quantization import QuantizationModifier

    # Configure the simple PTQ quantization
    recipe = QuantizationModifier(
        targets="Linear",
        scheme="FP8_DYNAMIC",
        ignore=["lm_head"],
    )

    # Apply the quantization algorithm.
    oneshot(model=model, recipe=recipe)

    # Save the model: Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-FP8-Dynamic
    SAVE_DIR = MODEL_ID.split("/")[1] + "-FP8-Dynamic"
    model.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR)
    tokenizer.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR)
<

3. Evaluating Accuracy        *llm_compressor-fp8-index-3-evaluating-accuracy*

Load and run the model in `vllm`:

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM("./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-FP8-Dynamic")
    result = llm.generate("Hello my name is")
    print(result[0].outputs[0].text)
<
Evaluate accuracy with `lm_eval` (for example on 250 samples of
`gsm8k`):

  Note: ~

  Quantized models can be sensitive to the presence of the `bos` token.
  `lm_eval` does not add a `bos` token by default, so make sure to
  include the `add_bos_token=True` argument when running your
  evaluations.

>bash
    MODEL=$PWD/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-FP8-Dynamic
    lm_eval \
      --model vllm \
      --model_args pretrained=$MODEL,add_bos_token=True \
      --tasks gsm8k  --num_fewshot 5 --batch_size auto --limit 250
<
Here's an example of the resulting scores:

>text
    |Tasks|Version|     Filter     |n-shot|  Metric   |   |Value|   |Stderr|
    | --- |------:| -------------- |-----:| --------- | - |----:| - |-----:|
    |gsm8k|      3|flexible-extract|     5|exact_match|↑  |0.768|±  |0.0268|
    |     |       |strict-match    |     5|exact_match|↑  |0.768|±  |0.0268|
<

                        *llm_compressor-fp8-index-troubleshooting-and-support*
TROUBLESHOOTING AND SUPPORT

If you encounter any issues or have feature requests, please open an
issue on the vllm-project/llm-compressor
https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/issues GitHub repository.

                        *llm_compressor-fp8-index-online-dynamic-quantization*
ONLINE DYNAMIC QUANTIZATION

Dynamic quantization of an original precision BF16/FP16 model to FP8 can
be achieved with vLLM without any calibration data required. You can
enable the feature by specifying `--quantization="fp8_per_tensor"` in
the command line or setting `quantization="fp8_per_tensor"` in the LLM
constructor.

In this mode, all Linear modules (except for the final `lm_head`) have
their weights quantized down to FP8_E4M3 precision with a per-tensor
scale. Activations have their minimum and maximum values calculated
during each forward pass to provide a dynamic per-tensor scale for high
accuracy. As a result, latency improvements are limited in this mode.

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM("facebook/opt-125m", quantization="fp8_per_tensor")
    # INFO 06-10 17:55:42 model_runner.py:157] Loading model weights took 0.1550 GB
    result = llm.generate("Hello, my name is")
    print(result[0].outputs[0].text)
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
LLM Compressor                        *vllm-quantization-llm_compressor-index*

LLM Compressor https://docs.vllm.ai/projects/llm-compressor/en/latest/
is a library for optimizing models for deployment with vLLM. It provides
a comprehensive set of quantization algorithms, including support for
techniques such as FP4, FP8, INT8, and INT4 quantization.

                    *quantization-llm_compressor-index-why-use-llm-compressor*
WHY USE LLM COMPRESSOR?

Modern LLMs often contain billions of parameters stored in 16-bit or
32-bit floating point, requiring substantial GPU memory and limiting
deployment options. Quantization lowers memory requirements while
maintaining inference output quality by reducing the precision of model
weights and activations to smaller data types.

LLM Compressor provides the following benefits:

- Reduced memory footprint: Run larger models on smaller GPUs.
- Lower inference costs: Serve more concurrent users per GPU, directly
  reducing the cost per query in production deployments.
- Faster inference: Smaller data types mean less memory bandwidth
  consumed, which often translates to higher throughput, especially for
  memory-bound workloads.

LLM Compressor handles the complexity of quantization, calibration, and
format conversion, producing models ready for immediate use with vLLM.

KEY FEATURES                  *quantization-llm_compressor-index-key-features*

- Multiple Quantization Algorithms: Support for AWQ, GPTQ, AutoRound,
  and Round-to-Nearest. Also includes support for QuIP and
  SpinQuant-style transforms as well as KV cache and attention
  quantization.
- Multiple Quantization Methods: Support for FP8, INT8, INT4, NVFP4,
  MXFP4, MXFP6, and mixed-precision quantization
- One-Shot Quantization: Quantize models quickly with minimal
  calibration data
- vLLM Integration: Seamlessly deploy quantized models with vLLM using
  the compressed-tensors format
- Hugging Face Compatibility: Works with models from the Hugging Face
  Hub

RESOURCES                        *quantization-llm_compressor-index-resources*

- LLM Compressor examples
  https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/tree/main/examples
- GitHub Repository https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
INT4 W4A16                                    *vllm-llm_compressor-int4-index*

vLLM supports quantizing weights to INT4 for memory savings and
inference acceleration. This quantization method is particularly useful
for reducing model size and maintaining low latency in workloads with
low queries per second (QPS).

Please visit the HF collection of
quantized INT4 checkpoints of popular LLMs ready to use with vLLM
https://huggingface.co/collections/neuralmagic/int4-llms-for-vllm-668ec34bf3c9fa45f857df2c.

  Note: ~

  INT4 computation is supported on NVIDIA GPUs with compute capability > 
  8.0 (Ampere, Ada Lovelace, Hopper, Blackwell).

PREREQUISITES                        *llm_compressor-int4-index-prerequisites*

To use INT4 quantization with vLLM, you'll need to install the
llm-compressor https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/ library:

>bash
    (venv-llm-compressor) pip install llmcompressor
<
Additionally, install `vllm` and `lm-evaluation-harness` for evaluation:

>bash
    (venv-vllm) pip install vllm "lm-eval[api]>=0.4.12"
<
Please use separate environments for vLLM and llm-compressor as they
might not work together.

QUANTIZATION PROCESS          *llm_compressor-int4-index-quantization-process*

The quantization process involves four main steps:

1. Loading the model
2. Preparing calibration data
3. Applying quantization
4. Evaluating accuracy in vLLM
1. Loading the Model           *llm_compressor-int4-index-1-loading-the-model*

Load your model and tokenizer using the standard `transformers`
AutoModel classes:

>python
    from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM

    MODEL_ID = "meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct"
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        MODEL_ID,
        device_map="auto",
        dtype="auto",
    )
    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID)
<

                      *llm_compressor-int4-index-2-preparing-calibration-data*
2. Preparing Calibration Data

When quantizing weights to INT4, you need sample data to estimate the
weight updates and calibrated scales. It's best to use calibration data
that closely matches your deployment data. For a general-purpose
instruction-tuned model, you can use a dataset like `ultrachat`:

>python
    from datasets import load_dataset

    NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES = 512
    MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH = 2048

    # Load and preprocess the dataset
    ds = load_dataset("HuggingFaceH4/ultrachat_200k", split="train_sft")
    ds = ds.shuffle(seed=42).select(range(NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES))

    def preprocess(example):
        return {"text": tokenizer.apply_chat_template(example["messages"], tokenize=False)}
    ds = ds.map(preprocess)

    def tokenize(sample):
        return tokenizer(sample["text"], padding=False, max_length=MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH, truncation=True, add_special_tokens=False)
    ds = ds.map(tokenize, remove_columns=ds.column_names)
<

3. Applying Quantization   *llm_compressor-int4-index-3-applying-quantization*

Now, apply the quantization algorithms:

>python
    from llmcompressor import oneshot
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.quantization import GPTQModifier
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.smoothquant import SmoothQuantModifier

    # Configure the quantization algorithms
    recipe = GPTQModifier(targets="Linear", scheme="W4A16", ignore=["lm_head"])

    # Apply quantization
    oneshot(
        model=model,
        dataset=ds,
        recipe=recipe,
        max_seq_length=MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH,
        num_calibration_samples=NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES,
    )

    # Save the compressed model: Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A16-G128
    SAVE_DIR = MODEL_ID.split("/")[1] + "-W4A16-G128"
    model.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR, save_compressed=True)
    tokenizer.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR)
<
This process creates a W4A16 model with weights quantized to 4-bit
integers.

4. Evaluating Accuracy       *llm_compressor-int4-index-4-evaluating-accuracy*

After quantization, you can load and run the model in vLLM:

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM("./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A16-G128")
<
To evaluate accuracy, you can use `lm_eval`:

>bash
    lm_eval --model vllm \
      --model_args pretrained="./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A16-G128",add_bos_token=true \
      --tasks gsm8k \
      --num_fewshot 5 \
      --limit 250 \
      --batch_size 'auto'
<
  Note: ~

  Quantized models can be sensitive to the presence of the `bos` token.
  Make sure to include the `add_bos_token=True` argument when running
  evaluations.

BEST PRACTICES                      *llm_compressor-int4-index-best-practices*

- Start with 512 samples for calibration data, and increase if accuracy
  drops
- Ensure the calibration data contains a high variety of samples to
  prevent overfitting towards a specific use case
- Use a sequence length of 2048 as a starting point
- Employ the chat template or instruction template that the model was
  trained with
- If you've fine-tuned a model, consider using a sample of your training
  data for calibration
- Tune key hyperparameters to the quantization algorithm:
  - `dampening_frac` sets how much influence the GPTQ algorithm has.
    Lower values can improve accuracy, but can lead to numerical
    instabilities that cause the algorithm to fail.
  - `actorder` sets the activation ordering. When compressing the
    weights of a layer weight, the order in which channels are quantized
    matters. Setting `actorder="weight"` can improve accuracy without
    added latency.

The following is an example of an expanded quantization recipe you can
tune to your own use case:

>python
    from compressed_tensors.quantization import (
        QuantizationArgs,
        QuantizationScheme,
        QuantizationStrategy,
        QuantizationType,
    )
    recipe = GPTQModifier(
        targets="Linear",
        config_groups={
            "config_group": QuantizationScheme(
                targets=["Linear"],
                weights=QuantizationArgs(
                    num_bits=4,
                    type=QuantizationType.INT,
                    strategy=QuantizationStrategy.GROUP,
                    group_size=128,
                    symmetric=True,
                    dynamic=False,
                    actorder="weight",
                ),
            ),
        },
        ignore=["lm_head"],
        update_size=NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES,
        dampening_frac=0.01,
    )
<

                       *llm_compressor-int4-index-troubleshooting-and-support*
TROUBLESHOOTING AND SUPPORT

If you encounter any issues or have feature requests, please open an
issue on the vllm-project/llm-compressor
https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/issues GitHub repository.
The full INT4 quantization example in `llm-compressor` is available here
https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/blob/main/examples/quantization_w4a16/llama3_example.py.

The quantization process involves four main steps:

1. Loading the model
2. Preparing calibration data
3. Applying quantization
4. Evaluating accuracy in vLLM
1. Loading the Model      *llm_compressor-int8_w4a8-index-1-loading-the-model*

Load your model and tokenizer using the standard `transformers`
AutoModel classes:

>python

    MODEL_ID = "meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct"

        MODEL_ID,
        dtype="auto",

    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID)
<

                 *llm_compressor-int8_w4a8-index-2-preparing-calibration-data*
2. Preparing Calibration Data

When quantizing activations to INT8 and weights to INT4, you need sample
data to estimate the activation scales. It's best to use calibration
data that closely matches your deployment data. For a general-purpose
instruction-tuned model, you can use a dataset like `ultrachat`:

>python
    from datasets import load_dataset

    NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES = 512
    MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH = 2048

    # Load and preprocess the dataset
    ds = load_dataset("HuggingFaceH4/ultrachat_200k", split="train_sft")
    ds = ds.shuffle(seed=42).select(range(NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES))

    def preprocess(example):
        return {"text": tokenizer.apply_chat_template(example["messages"], tokenize=False)}
    ds = ds.map(preprocess)

    def tokenize(sample):
        return tokenizer(sample["text"], padding=False, max_length=MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH, truncation=True, add_special_tokens=False)
    ds = ds.map(tokenize, remove_columns=ds.column_names)
<

                      *llm_compressor-int8_w4a8-index-3-applying-quantization*
3. Applying Quantization

Now, apply the quantization algorithms.

The following recipes create W4A8 models (int4 weights, int8
activations). On Arm® CPUs, this is accelerated through KleidiAI
https://github.com/ARM-software/kleidiai.

Use groupwise for best accuracy, and channelwise for best inference
performance.

GroupwiseChannelwise
>python
    from llmcompressor import oneshot
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.quantization import GPTQModifier

    # Configure the quantization algorithms
    recipe = [
        GPTQModifier(
            targets="Linear",
            scheme="W4A8",
            ignore=["lm_head"],
            dampening_frac=0.01
        ),
    ]

    # Apply quantization
    oneshot(
        model=model,
        dataset=ds,
        recipe=recipe,
        max_seq_length=MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH,
        num_calibration_samples=NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES,
    )

    # Save the compressed model: Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A8-G128-Dynamic-Per-Token
    SAVE_DIR = MODEL_ID.split("/")[1] + "-W4A8-G128-Dynamic-Per-Token"
    model.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR, save_compressed=True)
    tokenizer.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR)
<
>python
    from llmcompressor import oneshot
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.quantization import GPTQModifier
    from compressed_tensors.quantization import QuantizationStrategy, QuantizationType

    scheme = {
        "targets": ["Linear"],
        "weights": {
            "num_bits": 4,
            "type": QuantizationType.INT,
            "strategy": QuantizationStrategy.CHANNEL,
            "symmetric": True,
            "dynamic": False,
            "group_size": None,
        },
        "input_activations": {
            "num_bits": 8,
            "type": QuantizationType.INT,
            "strategy": QuantizationStrategy.TOKEN,
            "dynamic": True,
            "symmetric": False,
            "observer": None,
        },
        "output_activations": None,
    }

    recipe = [
        GPTQModifier(
            targets="Linear",
            config_groups={"group_0": scheme},
            ignore=["lm_head"],
            dampening_frac=0.01,
        ),
    ]

    oneshot(
        model=model,
        dataset=ds,
        recipe=recipe,
        max_seq_length=MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH,
        num_calibration_samples=NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES,
    )

    # Save the compressed model: Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A8-Channelwise-Dynamic-Per-Token
    SAVE_DIR = MODEL_ID.split("/")[1] + "-W4A8-Channelwise-Dynamic-Per-Token"
    model.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR, save_compressed=True)
    tokenizer.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR)
<

4. Evaluating Accuracy  *llm_compressor-int8_w4a8-index-4-evaluating-accuracy*

GroupwiseChannelwise
After quantization, you can load and run the model in vLLM:

        --num_fewshot 5 \
        --limit 250 \
        --batch_size 'auto'
<
After quantization, you can load and run the model in vLLM:

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM("./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A8-Channelwise-Dynamic-Per-Token")
<
To evaluate accuracy, you can use `lm_eval`:

>bash
    lm_eval --model vllm \
        --model_args pretrained="./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W4A8-Channelwise-Dynamic-Per-Token",add_bos_token=true \
        --tasks gsm8k \
        --num_fewshot 5 \
        --limit 250 \
        --batch_size 'auto'
<
  Note: ~

  Quantized models can be sensitive to the presence of the `bos` token.
  Make sure to include the `add_bos_token=True` argument when running
  evaluations.

BEST PRACTICES                 *llm_compressor-int8_w4a8-index-best-practices*

INT8 W8A8                                *vllm-llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index*

vLLM supports quantizing weights and activations to INT8 for memory
savings and inference acceleration. This quantization method is
particularly useful for reducing model size while maintaining good
performance.

Please visit the HF collection of
quantized INT8 checkpoints of popular LLMs ready to use with vLLM
https://huggingface.co/collections/neuralmagic/int8-llms-for-vllm-668ec32c049dca0369816415.

  Note: ~

  INT8 computation is supported on NVIDIA GPUs with compute capability > 
  7.5 (Turing, Ampere, Ada Lovelace, Hopper).

  Warning: ~

  Blackwell GPU Limitation: INT8 is not supported on compute capability
  >= 10.0 (e.g., RTX 6000 Blackwell). Use
  |vllm-llm_compressor-fp8-index| instead, or run on Hopper/Ada/Ampere
  architectures.

PREREQUISITES                   *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-prerequisites*

>bash
    (venv-vllm) pip install vllm "lm-eval[api]>=0.4.12"
<
Please use separate environments for vLLM and llm-compressor as they
might not work together.

QUANTIZATION PROCESS     *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-quantization-process*

The quantization process involves four main steps:

1. Loading the model
2. Preparing calibration data
3. Applying quantization
4. Evaluating accuracy in vLLM
1. Loading the Model      *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-1-loading-the-model*

AutoModel classes:

>python
    from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM

    MODEL_ID = "meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct"

        MODEL_ID,
        device_map="auto",
        dtype="auto",
    )
    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID)
<

                 *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-2-preparing-calibration-data*
2. Preparing Calibration Data

When quantizing activations to INT8, you need sample data to estimate
the activation scales. It's best to use calibration data that closely
matches your deployment data. For a general-purpose instruction-tuned
model, you can use a dataset like `ultrachat`:

>python
    from datasets import load_dataset

    NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES = 512
    MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH = 2048

    # Load and preprocess the dataset
    ds = load_dataset("HuggingFaceH4/ultrachat_200k", split="train_sft")
    ds = ds.shuffle(seed=42).select(range(NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES))

    def preprocess(example):
        return {"text": tokenizer.apply_chat_template(example["messages"], tokenize=False)}
    ds = ds.map(preprocess)

    ds = ds.map(tokenize, remove_columns=ds.column_names)
<

                      *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-3-applying-quantization*
3. Applying Quantization

Now, apply the quantization algorithms:

>python
    from llmcompressor import oneshot
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.quantization import GPTQModifier
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.smoothquant import SmoothQuantModifier

    # Configure the quantization algorithms
    recipe = [
        SmoothQuantModifier(smoothing_strength=0.8),
        GPTQModifier(targets="Linear", scheme="W8A8", ignore=["lm_head"]),
    ]

    # Apply quantization
    oneshot(
        model=model,
        dataset=ds,
        recipe=recipe,
        max_seq_length=MAX_SEQUENCE_LENGTH,
        num_calibration_samples=NUM_CALIBRATION_SAMPLES,
    )

    # Save the compressed model: Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W8A8-Dynamic-Per-Token
    SAVE_DIR = MODEL_ID.split("/")[1] + "-W8A8-Dynamic-Per-Token"
    model.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR, save_compressed=True)
    tokenizer.save_pretrained(SAVE_DIR)
<
This process creates a W8A8 model with weights and activations quantized
to 8-bit integers.

4. Evaluating Accuracy  *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-4-evaluating-accuracy*

After quantization, you can load and run the model in vLLM:

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM("./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W8A8-Dynamic-Per-Token")
<
To evaluate accuracy, you can use `lm_eval`:

>bash
    lm_eval --model vllm \
      --model_args pretrained="./Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct-W8A8-Dynamic-Per-Token",add_bos_token=true \
      --tasks gsm8k \
      --num_fewshot 5 \
      --limit 250 \
      --batch_size 'auto'
<
  Note: ~

  Quantized models can be sensitive to the presence of the `bos` token.
  Make sure to include the `add_bos_token=True` argument when running
  evaluations.

BEST PRACTICES                 *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-best-practices*

- Start with 512 samples for calibration data (increase if accuracy
  drops)
- Use a sequence length of 2048 as a starting point
- Employ the chat template or instruction template that the model was
  trained with
- If you've fine-tuned a model, consider using a sample of your training
  data for calibration

                  *llm_compressor-int8_w8a8-index-troubleshooting-and-support*
TROUBLESHOOTING AND SUPPORT

If you encounter any issues or have feature requests, please open an
issue on the vllm-project/llm-compressor
https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/issues GitHub repository.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
NVIDIA Model Optimizer                    *vllm-quantization-modelopt-index-2*

The NVIDIA Model Optimizer https://github.com/NVIDIA/Model-Optimizer is
a library designed to optimize models for inference with NVIDIA GPUs. It
includes tools for Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) and Quantization
Aware Training (QAT) of Large Language Models (LLMs), Vision Language
Models (VLMs), and diffusion models.

We recommend installing the library with:

>bash
    pip install nvidia-modelopt
<

         *quantization-modelopt-index-2-supported-modelopt-checkpoint-formats*
SUPPORTED MODELOPT CHECKPOINT FORMATS

vLLM detects ModelOpt checkpoints via `hf_quant_config.json` and
supports the following `quantization.quant_algo` values:

- `FP8`: per-tensor weight scale (+ optional static activation scale).
- `FP8_PER_CHANNEL_PER_TOKEN`: per-channel weight scale and dynamic
  per-token activation quantization.
- `FP8_PB_WO` (ModelOpt may emit `fp8_pb_wo`): block-scaled FP8
  weight-only (typically 128×128 blocks).
- `NVFP4`: ModelOpt NVFP4 checkpoints (use
  `quantization="modelopt_fp4"`).
- `W4A16_NVFP4`: weight-only ModelOpt NVFP4 checkpoints with 16-bit
  activations (use `quantization="modelopt_fp4"`).
- `MXFP8`: ModelOpt MXFP8 checkpoints (use
  `quantization="modelopt_mxfp8"`).
- `INT8_PB_WO`: block-scaled INT8 weight-only linear layers and MoE
  experts with 16-bit activations (use `quantization="modelopt_int8"`):
  a symmetric int8 `weight` and a `weight_scale` per `group_size` input
  elements (for example 16 or 32). `group_size` is required. vLLM serves
  these layers with its mixed-precision (WNA16) kernels, such as Marlin
  and Humming on NVIDIA and Triton on ROCm. MoE experts run on NVIDIA
  GPUs only, on the WNA16 MoE kernels (Marlin, Triton, or Humming with
  `--moe-backend humming`).
- `MIXED_PRECISION`: per-layer formats specified by `quantized_layers`
  (use `quantization="modelopt_mixed"`). Each layer may use any format
  above; `INT8_PB_WO` layers take their own `group_size`. `INT8_PB_WO`
  routed experts must use it for every expert and projection of their
  MoE layer, with one `group_size`. A token embedding marked
  `W4A16_NVFP4` stays packed and only the looked-up rows are
  dequantized; the model must build its embedding with the checkpoint
  prefix, as Nemotron-H does. Such an embedding cannot be tied to the LM
  head, so checkpoints that tie it are rejected.

For per-tensor `FP8`, vLLM converts E4M3FN weights and scales to
E4M3FNUZ when required by the GPU, for example on AMD MI300.

For Qwen3-based DFlash and DSpark drafts, `quantized_layers` and
exclusion lists use the exported checkpoint's module names. vLLM maps
these names to the draft's module prefix and decoder layer indices
before constructing quantized layers.

  Note: ~

  For NVFP4 checkpoints, vLLM selects a GEMM kernel automatically at
  load time from the backends available on the current platform
  (CUTLASS, FlashInfer, Marlin, and others). On GPUs without a supported
  native FP4 GEMM kernel, vLLM falls back to weight-only (W4A16)
  execution via Marlin and logs a warning; this may reduce throughput
  for compute-heavy workloads. Use `--linear-backend` to override the
  automatic selection (this replaces the deprecated
  `VLLM_NVFP4_GEMM_BACKEND` environment variable). Values relevant to
  NVFP4 include `cutlass`, `flashinfer_cutlass`, `flashinfer_cutedsl`,
  `flashinfer_trtllm`, `flashinfer_cudnn`, and `marlin`; see
  |vllm.config.kernel.KernelConfig| on the
  |vllm-configuration-engine_args-index| page and shown by
  `vllm serve --help=KernelConfig`.

  Note: ~

  For models quantized to MXFP8 with BF16 activations on SM100-family
  GPUs, use `--linear-backend flashinfer_trtllm` to select FlashInfer's
  TensorRT-LLM GEMM backend.

For Muse Spark NVFP4 on NVIDIA, the prepared-input FlashInfer TRT-LLM
path skips CUDA-graph padding rows when routing runs outside the expert
kernel. The router writes expert IDs of `-1` and zero weights in its
existing fused kernel. This requires a padding mask matching the routed
token layout; other dispatch paths retain their backend-specific
behavior. Set `VLLM_MOE_SKIP_PADDING=0` before starting the engine to
disable this optimization for comparisons.

               *quantization-modelopt-index-2-benchmarking-with-dummy-weights*
BENCHMARKING WITH DUMMY WEIGHTS

For performance experiments with random weights, use
`--load-format dummy`. FP8 weights get random codes across the FP8
range, as checkpoints use it, so compute-bound steps draw realistic
power (a narrow range runs faster than real weights on power-capped
GPUs). ModelOpt NVFP4 MoE layers initialize positive quantization scales
with matching gate/up global scales, including for FlashInfer MegaMoE
(`--moe-backend flashinfer_moe_ep_cutedsl`). On SM107 GPUs, see
|serving-expert_parallel_deployment-index-flashinfer-megamoe-on-sm107|.
Muse Spark's NVIDIA `deep_gemm_mega_moe` backend also supports dummy
loading: it initializes valid NVFP4 scales and finalizes its expert
weights and residual constants before the first forward. Enable expert
parallelism for this backend. Dummy weights are for benchmarking, not
model quality evaluation. Normal checkpoint loading uses the
checkpoint's scales.

        *quantization-modelopt-index-2-quantizing-huggingface-models-with-ptq*
QUANTIZING HUGGINGFACE MODELS WITH PTQ

You can quantize HuggingFace models using the example scripts provided
in the Model Optimizer repository. The primary script for LLM PTQ is
typically found within the `examples/llm_ptq` directory.

Below is an example showing how to quantize a model using modelopt's PTQ
API:

  Code: ~

  >python
      import modelopt.torch.quantization as mtq
      from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM

      # Load the model from HuggingFace
      model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("<path_or_model_id>")

      # Select the quantization config, for example, FP8
      config = mtq.FP8_DEFAULT_CFG

      # Define a forward loop function for calibration
      def forward_loop(model):
          for data in calib_set:
              model(data)

      # PTQ with in-place replacement of quantized modules
      model = mtq.quantize(model, config, forward_loop)
<

After the model is quantized, you can export it to a quantized
checkpoint using the export API:

>python
    import torch
    from modelopt.torch.export import export_hf_checkpoint

    with torch.inference_mode():
        export_hf_checkpoint(
            model,  # The quantized model.
            export_dir,  # The directory where the exported files will be stored.
        )
<
The quantized checkpoint can then be deployed with vLLM. As an example,
the following code shows how to deploy
`nvidia/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct-FP8`, which is the FP8 quantized
checkpoint derived from `meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct`, using vLLM:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

      def main():
          model_id = "nvidia/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct-FP8"

          # Ensure you specify quantization="modelopt" when loading the modelopt checkpoint
          llm = LLM(model=model_id, quantization="modelopt", trust_remote_code=True)

          sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.8, top_p=0.9)

          prompts = [
              "Hello, my name is",
              "The president of the United States is",
              "The capital of France is",
              "The future of AI is",
          ]

          outputs = llm.generate(prompts, sampling_params)

          for output in outputs:
              prompt = output.prompt
              generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
              print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}, Generated text: {generated_text!r}")

      if __name__ == "__main__":
          main()
<

          *quantization-modelopt-index-2-running-the-openai-compatible-server*
RUNNING THE OPENAI-COMPATIBLE SERVER

To serve a local ModelOpt checkpoint via the OpenAI-compatible API:

>bash
    vllm serve <path_to_exported_checkpoint> \
      --quantization modelopt \
      --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000
<

                     *quantization-modelopt-index-2-testing-local-checkpoints*
TESTING (LOCAL CHECKPOINTS)

vLLM's ModelOpt unit tests are gated by local checkpoint paths and are
skipped by default in CI. To run the tests locally:

>bash
    export VLLM_TEST_MODELOPT_FP8_PC_PT_MODEL_PATH=<path_to_fp8_pc_pt_checkpoint>
    export VLLM_TEST_MODELOPT_FP8_PB_WO_MODEL_PATH=<path_to_fp8_pb_wo_checkpoint>
    pytest -q tests/quantization/test_modelopt.py
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Online Quantization                         *vllm-quantization-online-index-2*

Online quantization lets you take a BF16/FP16 model and quantize its
Linear and MoE weights, and optionally its lm_head, to lower precision
(such as FP8) at load time, without needing a pre-quantized checkpoint
or calibration data. Weights are converted during model loading and
activations are dynamically scaled during each forward pass.

QUICK START                          *quantization-online-index-2-quick-start*

Pass a scheme name to the `quantization` parameter:

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    # Per-tensor FP8 quantization (one scale per weight tensor)
    llm = LLM("meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B", quantization="fp8_per_tensor")

    # Per-block FP8 quantization (128x128 block scaling for weights and 1x128 block scaling for activations)
    llm = LLM("meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B", quantization="fp8_per_block")

    # MXFP8 quantization for weights and activations
    llm = LLM("meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B", quantization="mxfp8")

    # MXFP4 weight; activation quantization depends on the `linear_backend` picked
    llm = LLM("meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B", quantization="mxfp4")

    # MXFP4 MOE-only weight and activation quantization
    llm = LLM(
        "Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B",
        quantization="mxfp4",
        quantization_config={"linear": {"activation": None, "weight": None}}
    )
<
Or with the CLI:

>bash
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B --quantization fp8_per_tensor
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B --quantization fp8_per_block
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B --quantization mxfp8
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B --quantization mxfp4
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B --quantization nvfp4_weight_only

    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B --quantization mxfp4 \
        --quantization-config '{"linear":{"activation":null,"weight":null}}'

    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B --quantization lut_b_moe
<
  Note: ~

  `--cpu-offload-gb` does not offload the weights that online
  quantization creates: they stay in GPU memory. Only layers that are
  not quantized online are offloaded. When `--cpu-offload-gb` offloads
  the MoE experts (`--cpu-offload-params` empty or matching them),
  `nvfp4_per_token` stages the full-precision experts on the CPU and
  moves them to the GPU one expert at a time to quantize them.

SUPPORTED SCHEMES              *quantization-online-index-2-supported-schemes*

            *quantization-online-index-2-rl-weight-updates-with-direct-reload*
RL WEIGHT UPDATES WITH DIRECT RELOAD

Online-quantized models accept in-place weight updates
(`reload_weights`, RL weight sync): the trainer sends BF16 weights under
their checkpoint names. With the `VLLM_RELOAD_MODE` environment variable
set to `direct`, each tensor is quantized as it is loaded;
`fp8_per_tensor`, `fp8_per_block`, `fp8_per_channel` and `mxfp8` are
supported on linear and MoE layers, and `mxfp4`
(`--quantization-config.moe mxfp4`) and `nvfp4_per_token` on MoE layers.
See |training-direct_reload-index-online-quantization| for the refused
schemes and the NVFP4 delivery rule.

ADVANCED CONFIGURATION    *quantization-online-index-2-advanced-configuration*

For fine-grained control, use a `quantization_config` dictionary.

Schema                                    *quantization-online-index-2-schema*

>yaml
    quantization_config:
      linear:
        weight: <name>      # see QUANT_KEY_NAMES in vllm/config/quantization.py
        activation: <name>
        hadamard_size: 32   # optional power of two, so far mxfp4 only: see `mxfp4_hadamard32`
      moe:
        weight: <name>
        activation: <name>
      ignore: [<layer-name-or-regex-or-fnmatch-pattern>, ...]
<
`linear` and `moe` accept a full `{weight, activation}` dict, or a bare
string. A string resolves first against the `--quantization` shorthands
(taking the matching layer-kind slot), then against `QUANT_KEY_NAMES` as
a weight name. Unset fields fall back to the `--quantization`
shorthand's defaults, or for already-quantized checkpoints to whatever
the checkpoint declares. An explicitly configured `activation: null` is
not the same as an omitted activation: it requests unquantized (BF16)
activations instead of the method's default.

On XPU, non-block FP8 scaled-mm linear layers default to W8A16; setting
`--linear-backend xpu` forces W8A8. Use `--linear-backend xpu_woq` to
explicitly select weight-only quantization (W8A16). Setting
`--linear-backend torch` also forces W8A8 but runs the GEMM through
`torch._scaled_mm` instead of the custom XPU kernel.

The CLI accepts the same shape as JSON or as dotted keys:

>bash
    vllm serve <model> --quantization-config '{"moe":{"activation":"mxfp8"}}'
    vllm serve <model> --quantization-config.moe.activation mxfp8
<

Activation overrides        *quantization-online-index-2-activation-overrides*

`quantization_config` lets you select an activation format independently
of the weight format. For online quantization, it can be used to
override the default activation quantization of MXFP4 layers. Online
MXFP8 MoE layers accept only `"activation": "mxfp8"`, their default;
other online weight formats reject an activation override.

For example, to enable MXFP4 MoE weights along MXFP8 activations:

>bash
    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B --quantization online \
        --quantization-config '{"moe":{"weight":"mxfp4","activation":"mxfp8"}}'
<
For pre-quantized models, the supported activation overrides are
specific to each weight quantization type; today this is for example
available for MXFP4 MoE checkpoints where you can opt into e.g. FP8
activations:

>bash
    vllm serve openai/gpt-oss-20b --quantization-config.moe.activation mxfp8
<
Combine with `--moe-backend` to pin a specific kernel family.

For MXFP4 linear layers on RDNA3/RDNA3.5 (gfx11), `int8_per_group_32`
selects the W4A8 int8-dot GEMV for decode (batches of up to 8 tokens);
larger batches run weight-only. On W4A4 checkpoints this replaces the
MXFP4 activation quantization:

>bash
    vllm serve <mxfp4-model> --quantization-config.linear.activation int8_per_group_32
<
Likewise, for int4 (GPTQ, AWQ, compressed-tensors WNA16) MoE checkpoints
on RDNA3/RDNA3.5 (gfx11), `int8_per_group_32` selects the
`rdna3_int4_int8` MoE backend, which decodes batches of up to 64 tokens
with int8 activations; larger batches run the Triton WNA16 kernels with
16-bit activations:

>bash
    vllm serve <int4-moe-model> --quantization-config.moe.activation int8_per_group_32
<

   *quantization-online-index-2-re-quantizing-layers-to-a-different-precision*
Re-quantizing layers to a different precision

An online target can re-quantize a checkpoint-quantized (prequantized)
layer at load time.

vLLM uses the checkpoint method to load and dequantize the checkpoint
weight, and then applies the online quantization method. This requires
the checkpoint method to implement weight dequantization, unsupported
source and target combinations raise an error.

Currently supported combinations are:

Checkpoint layer           Online target             Requirements
-------------------------  ------------------------  -------------------------
Serialized ModelOpt MXFP8  any online linear method  Target-specific hardware
linear                                               requirements
Serialized ModelOpt NVFP4  any online linear method  Target-specific hardware
linear                                               requirements
Serialized                 any online MoE method     Target-specific hardware
compressed-tensors NVFP4                             requirements
MoE
Serialized ModelOpt NVFP4  any online MoE method     Target-specific hardware
MoE                                                  requirements
Serialized Quark NVFP4     any online MoE method     Target-specific hardware
MoE                                                  requirements
Serialized FP8 MoE with    any online MoE method     Target-specific hardware
dynamic activation                                   requirements
quantization (fp8,
compressed-tensors,
Quark; per-tensor,
per-channel or block
weight scales)

For example, serialized ModelOpt MXFP8 linears can be converted to
per-channel-weight/per-token-activation FP8 at load time:

>bash
    vllm serve MiniMaxAI/MiniMax-M3-MXFP8 \
      --linear-backend auto \
      --quantization-config.linear fp8_per_channel
<
Similarly, for example, NVFP4 MoE checkpoints can be re-quantized to any
online MoE method, for example compressed-tensors NVFP4 experts to
online MXFP4:

>bash
    vllm serve RedHatAI/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 \
      --quantization-config '{"targets":{"*mlp.experts*":"mxfp4"}}'
<
For ModelOpt NVFP4 checkpoints, `--quantization mxfp4` retains the
checkpoint loader and, by default, converts eligible linear and MoE
layers, including attention projections and router gates. Use `targets`
to select specific layers instead, for example:

>bash
    vllm serve ricursiveAI/Ripple-65B-Preview-NVFP4 \
      --quantization mxfp4 --quantization-config '{"targets":{"*mlp*":"mxfp4"}}'
<
Unconverted checkpoint-quantized layers require native support for their
checkpoint format. NVFP4-to-MXFP4 conversion can reduce accuracy;
validate it per model or consider MXFP8 for dense and attention linears.

  Info: ~

  Re-quantization is available for both linear and MoE layers.

  Checkpoint-exclusion policy is not enforced by online
  quantization/re-quantization. Only exclusion through
  `--quantization-config.targets`/`--quantization-config.ignore` is
  enforced.

*quantization-online-index-2-online-quantization-on-unquantized-layers-from-partially-quantized-checkpoints*
Online quantization on unquantized layers from partially-quantized
checkpoints

Online quantization can be used on already quantized checkpoints
independently of their original `quant_method` (`modelopt`,
`compressed-tensors`, `quark`, etc.), for layers that are left
unquantized in the original checkpoint.

The checkpoint `quant_method` remains responsible for its quantized
layers, while the selected unquantized layers use the requested online
method.

For example:

>bash
    vllm serve amd/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B-MXFP4 \
      --quantization-config.linear mxfp8
<
adds MXFP8 quantization to the dense linear layers of a Quark checkpoint
where only MOE experts are quantized.

Similarly, the Quark MXFP4 checkpoint of Muse Spark Open keeps the
routed experts of layers 0 and 61 in BF16. On ROCm, this rounds them to
OCP MXFP4 at load, so every MoE layer runs the same AITER W4A4 experts
(an opt-in numerics change):

>bash
    vllm serve amd/Muse-Spark-Open-816B-A42B-Quark-MXFP4 \
      --quantization-config '{"targets":{"*layers.0.feed_forward.experts*":"mxfp4","*layers.61.feed_forward.experts*":"mxfp4"}}'
<
  Info: ~

  `quantization_config.ignore` is an online-only exclusion: the original
  `quant_method` relies solely on its own ignore implementation and on
  the ignored layers specified in `config.json`.

Quantizing the lm_head    *quantization-online-index-2-quantizing-the-lm_head*

The lm_head
(|vllm.model_executor.layers.vocab_parallel_embedding.ParallelLMHead|)
is neither a linear nor an MoE layer, but online quantization can
quantize an fp16/bf16 lm_head at load time with the online linear
methods. Decode reads the whole lm_head every step, so on
bandwidth-bound GPUs a full-precision head can be one of the largest
per-token costs, even when a checkpoint quantizes every other linear
layer.

Select the lm_head with a
|quantization-online-index-2-fine-grained-per-layer-quantization-schemes|
pattern that contains its module name. Patterns match the full layer
name, so `"lm_head"` only selects a top-level head; use
`"re:.*lm_head$"` for models that nest it, such as
`language_model.lm_head` in multimodal wrappers. Broad patterns such as
`"re:.*"`, and the `linear` field, do not select it. For example:

Target value             Recipe                    Notes
-----------------------  ------------------------  ---------------------------
{"weight":               fp8_e4m3 data,            "fp8_per_channel" (W8A8) is
"fp8_per_channel_        per-output-channel        also accepted; other FP8
static",                 scale, BF16 activations   schemes are not. CUDA and
"activation": null}                                XPU only, use
                                                   "fp8_per_channel" on ROCm.
{"weight":               uint4 data, one scale     lm_head only. Weight-only:
"int4_per_group_32_      and zero point per 32     "activation" may be omitted
asym"}                   input channels, BF16      or null. Runs on the
                         activations               platform's mixed-precision
                                                   (W4A16) linear kernel, e.g.
                                                   RDNAHybridW4A16LinearKernel
                                                   on gfx11/gfx12.

>bash
    # Quark int4 checkpoint whose lm_head (language_model.lm_head) is left in bf16
    vllm serve amd/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B-Quark-UINT4-G128 \
        --quantization-config '{"targets":{"re:.*lm_head$":{"weight":"int4_per_group_32_asym"}}}'
<
- The checkpoint must leave the lm_head unquantized: online
  re-quantization of a checkpoint-quantized lm_head raises
  `NotImplementedError`.
- `targets` is mutually exclusive with `linear` and `moe`. To also
  quantize the linear layers online, add patterns for them to `targets`.
- This quantizes the model's own lm_head. It is unrelated to
  `draft_lm_head_quantization` in `--speculative-config`, which gives a
  speculative-decoding drafter a private quantized copy of the target's
  lm_head and leaves the target unchanged. Both live in
  `vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization/online/lm_head.py`, where
  |vllm.model_executor.layers.quantization.online.lm_head.OnlineLMHeadMethodBase|
  implements the weight-only methods above that are specific to the
  lm_head.

  Warning: ~

  A lm_head tied to the input embeddings (e.g. small Qwen3 and Gemma
  models) keeps the embeddings' fp16/bf16 weight and adds the
  `int4_per_group_32_asym` copy next to it. It reduces the weight bytes
  read per decode step, but increases memory instead of saving it; the
  extra size is logged at load. The other online methods reject a tied
  lm_head.

  A tied `int4_per_group_32_asym` lm_head is not updated by in-place
  weight reloading (`reload_weights`, RL weight sync): the head keeps
  the copy quantized at load while the embeddings take the new weights.
  Do not use it with weight updates.

       *quantization-online-index-2-separate-schemes-for-dense-and-moe-layers*
Separate Schemes for Dense and MoE Layers

You can apply different quantization schemes to dense linear layers and
MoE expert layers via the `linear` and `moe` fields. Each accepts either
a full spec dict, or a bare string naming an online shorthand (e.g.
`"fp8_per_block"`) or weight format (e.g. `"fp8_per_block_static"`);
fields not set fall back to the shorthand defaults.

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    # Linear: per-block FP8; MoE: per-tensor FP8 (inherited from the shorthand)
    llm = LLM(
        "ibm-granite/granite-3.0-1b-a400m-base",
        quantization="fp8_per_tensor",
        quantization_config={
            "linear": "fp8_per_block",
        },
    )
<
Or,

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    # Linear: per-tensor FP8 (inherited); MoE: per-block FP8
    llm = LLM(
        "ibm-granite/granite-3.0-1b-a400m-base",
        quantization="fp8_per_tensor",
        quantization_config={
            "moe": "fp8_per_block",
        },
    )
<

              *quantization-online-index-2-excluding-layers-from-quantization*
Excluding Layers from Quantization

Use the `ignore` parameter to skip specific layers. It accepts exact
layer names, regex patterns (prefixed with `re:`), and patterns
understood by `fnmatch.fnmatch`
https://docs.python.org/3/library/fnmatch.html#fnmatch.fnmatch:

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM(
        "ibm-granite/granite-3.0-1b-a400m-base",
        quantization="fp8_per_tensor",
        quantization_config={
            "ignore": [
                # exact layer name
                "model.layers.1.self_attn.o_proj",
                # regex: skip all QKV projections
                "re:.*[qkv]_proj",
                # fnmatch: skip all MoE experts
                "*mlp.experts*",
            ],
        },
    )
<
  Note: ~

  For fused layers (e.g., `qkv_proj` which fuses `q_proj`, `k_proj`,
  `v_proj`), patterns may match the fused name directly or all of its
  unfused shard names.

     *quantization-online-index-2-fine-grained-per-layer-quantization-schemes*
Fine-Grained Per-Layer Quantization Schemes

Use the `targets` parameter to apply different online shorthands to
different layers, instead of one scheme applied everywhere via
`linear`/`moe`. Keys are exact layer names, regex patterns (prefixed
with `re:`), or patterns understood by `fnmatch.fnmatch`
https://docs.python.org/3/library/fnmatch.html#fnmatch.fnmatch; values
are shorthand names (`fp8_per_tensor`, `fp8_per_block`,
`fp8_per_channel`, `mxfp8`, `mxfp4`, `mxfp4_hadamard32`,
`int8_per_channel_weight_only`, `nvfp4_per_token`, `nvfp4_weight_only`)
or a `{weight, activation}` spec, which allows overriding the activation
quantization of a single layer.

Example: ~

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM(
        "Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B",
        quantization="online",
        quantization_config={
            "targets": {
                # exact layer name
                "model.layers.0.self_attn.o_proj": "fp8_per_tensor",
                # regex: quantize all QKV projections
                r"re:.*self_attn\.qkv_proj.*": "mxfp8",
                # fnmatch: quantize all MoE experts
                "*mlp.experts*": "mxfp4",
                # spec form: MXFP4 experts with BF16 (unquantized) activations
                "*shared_expert*": {"weight": "mxfp4", "activation": None},
            },
        },
    )
<
Or with the CLI:

>bash
    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B \
      --quantization online \
      --quantization-config '{"targets":{"model.layers.0.self_attn.o_proj":"fp8_per_tensor","re:.*self_attn\\.qkv_proj.*":"mxfp8","*mlp.experts*":"mxfp4"}}'
<
  Info: ~

  - `targets` is mutually exclusive with online `linear` and `moe`: set
    one or the other, not both.
  - A layer that matches no `targets` pattern is left unchanged from its
    checkpoint dtype.
  - A layer name may not match both `targets` and `ignore`, raising an
    error.
  - A layer may not match more than one `targets` pattern, raising an
    error.
  - The lm_head
    (|vllm.model_executor.layers.vocab_parallel_embedding.ParallelLMHead|)
    is neither a linear nor an MoE layer. It is quantized only when the
    text of a `targets` pattern contains its module name (`lm_head`), so
    `"re:.*"` and `"*"` do not select it, for example weight-only FP8:
    `"re:.*lm_head$": {"weight": "fp8_per_channel_static", "activation": None}`.
    See |quantization-online-index-2-quantizing-the-lm_head|.
  - The lm_head supports per-channel FP8 and `int4_per_group_32_asym`
    only: `fp8_per_channel` (W8A8), `fp8_per_channel_static` with
    `"activation": None` (weight-only; CUDA and XPU only, use
    `fp8_per_channel` on ROCm), or `int4_per_group_32_asym`
    (weight-only). Other schemes, and a lm_head the checkpoint already
    quantizes, raise an error; so does a tied lm_head, except with
    `int4_per_group_32_asym`. It cannot be combined with
    `draft_lm_head_quantization` or with models that read
    `lm_head.weight` directly (Gemma4 MTP, DiffusionGemma, K2Horizon).
    Models converted to sequence classification raise an error.
  - `"activation": None` on `fp8_per_channel_static` is valid for any
    linear layer; MoE experts reject it.
  - fnmatch-style patterns are supported only by online quantization;
    they are not applied to Quark or compressed-tensors configurations.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Quantized KV Cache                 *vllm-quantization-quantized_kvcache-index*

                  *quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-fp8-kv-cache-overview*
FP8 KV CACHE OVERVIEW

Efficient memory usage is crucial for working with large language
models. Quantizing the KV (Key-Value) cache to FP8 format can
significantly reduce its memory footprint. This optimization enables you
to store more tokens in memory, leading to improved throughput and
support for longer context windows.

  Note: When using the Flash Attention 3 backend with FP8 KV cache,
  attention operations are also performed in the quantized (FP8) domain.
  In this configuration, queries are quantized to FP8 in addition to
  keys and values.

*quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-supported-fp8-kv-cache-quantization-schemes*
Supported FP8 KV-Cache Quantization Schemes

vLLM supports two main quantization strategies for the FP8 KV-cache:

- Per-tensor quantization:
  A single scale is applied for each Q, K, and V tensor individually.
  (`q/k/v_scale = [1]`)
- Per-attention-head quantization:
  Each scale corresponds to an attention head: `q_scale = [num_heads]`,
  `k/v_scale = [num_kv_heads]`.

  Note: ~

  Per-attention-head quantization is currently available only with the
  Flash Attention backend and requires the calibration pathway provided
  by llm-compressor.

           *quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-scale-calibration-approaches*
Scale Calibration Approaches

You can configure how the quantization scales are computed in vLLM using
three different approaches:

1. No calibration (default scales):
   All quantization scales are set to `1.0`.
   Configure with:

   >python
       kv_cache_dtype="fp8"
<

2. [Recommended] Calibration with a dataset (via llm-compressor):
   Scales are estimated using a curated calibration dataset for maximum
   accuracy.
   This requires the llm-compressor
   https://github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor library.
   See example below!

      *quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-additional-kv_cache_dtype-options*
Additional `kv_cache_dtype` Options

- `kv_cache_dtype="auto"`: Use the model's default data type
- `kv_cache_dtype="fp8_e4m3"`: Supported on CUDA 11.8+ and ROCm (AMD
  GPUs)
- `kv_cache_dtype="fp8_e5m2"`: Supported on CUDA 11.8+

*quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-skipping-specific-layers-from-kv-cache-quantization*
Skipping Specific Layers from KV-Cache Quantization

Some attention layer types (e.g. sliding-window) are more sensitive to
KV-cache quantization. The `--kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers` flag leaves
the specified layers at the model's native dtype while keeping the rest
of the layers under the chosen quantized dtype. The flag accepts either
layer indices or layer-type names:

>bash
    # Skip every sliding-window attention layer.
    vllm serve <model> \
      --kv-cache-dtype fp8 \
      --kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers sliding_window

    # Skip specific layer indices.
    vllm serve <model> \
      --kv-cache-dtype fp8 \
      --kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers 0 1 23
<
When skipped layers create a mixed-precision KV cache, vLLM
automatically clears newly allocated attention blocks before use. With
pipeline parallelism, this behavior is coordinated across all stages,
including stages whose local KV cache uses only one precision.

Programmatic usage:

>python
    llm = LLM(
        model="meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct",
        kv_cache_dtype="fp8",
        kv_cache_dtype_skip_layers=["sliding_window"],
    )
<
 * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * *

EXAMPLES                       *quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-examples*

     *quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-1-no-calibration-kv_cache_dtypefp8*
1. No Calibration (`kv_cache_dtype="fp8"`)

All quantization scales are set to 1.0.

>python
    from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

    sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.7, top_p=0.8)
    llm = LLM(
        model="meta-llama/Llama-2-7b-chat-hf",
        kv_cache_dtype="fp8",
    )
    prompt = "London is the capital of"
    out = llm.generate(prompt, sampling_params)[0].outputs[0].text
    print(out)
<
 * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * * *

*quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-2-recommended-calibration-using-a-dataset-with-llm-compressor*
2. [Recommended] Calibration Using a Dataset (with `llm-compressor`)

For the highest-quality quantization, we recommend calibrating against a
dataset using `llm-compressor`. This enables advanced strategies such as
per-attention-head quantization.

           *quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-install-the-required-package*
Install the required package

>bash
    pip install llmcompressor
<

*quantization-quantized_kvcache-index-example-quantize-llama-attention-kv-cache-to-fp8*
Example: Quantize Llama Attention & KV Cache to FP8

>python
    """
    Quantize Llama attention + KV cache to FP8 (choose either 'tensor' or 'attn_head' strategy)
    using llm-compressor one-shot calibration.
    """

    from datasets import load_dataset
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer

    from llmcompressor import oneshot
    from llmcompressor.modifiers.quantization import QuantizationModifier
    from compressed_tensors.quantization import QuantizationScheme, QuantizationArgs

    # -----------------------------
    # Config
    # -----------------------------
    MODEL_ID = "meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct"
    DATASET_ID = "HuggingFaceH4/ultrachat_200k"
    DATASET_SPLIT = "train_sft"
