`num_draft_tokens >= num_verified_draft_tokens >= num_accepted_draft_tokens`,
both in total and at each step.

With `detailed`, ordered arrays are added, one entry per verification
step:

Field              Description
-----------------  -----------------------------------------------------------
per_step_accepted  Accepted draft count at each step.
per_step_drafted   Proposed draft count at each step. Records the effective
                   proposal length per step, so variable-length drafting (e.g.
                   adaptive speculation) is represented without a schema
                   change.
per_step_verified  Verified draft count at each step.

`metrics.speculative_decoding` is present whenever
`--per-request-spec-decode-metrics` is `summary`/`detailed`, speculative
decoding is enabled, and `n == 1` (with an all-zero histogram if the
request drafted nothing). It is `null` otherwise.

STREAMING            *speculative_decoding-acceptance_metrics-index-streaming*

In streaming responses, `metrics` (including `speculative_decoding`)
rides the final usage chunk, which is only emitted when usage reporting
is enabled --- set `stream_options.include_usage: true` or start the
server with `--enable-force-include-usage`.

*speculative_decoding-acceptance_metrics-index-relationship-to-prometheus-metrics*
RELATIONSHIP TO PROMETHEUS METRICS

The per-request fields are the individual-request counterpart of the
server-aggregated spec-decode counters at `/metrics`. Summed across the
single-sequence requests that report them, they reconcile with the
aggregate counters (which also count `n > 1` requests, so the totals
match only for all-`n == 1` workloads):

Per-request field (summed)     Prometheus counter
-----------------------------  -----------------------------------------------
num_spec_steps                 vllm:spec_decode_num_drafts_total
num_draft_tokens               vllm:spec_decode_num_draft_tokens_total
num_accepted_draft_tokens      vllm:spec_decode_num_accepted_tokens_total
num_verified_draft_tokens      vllm:spec_decode_num_verified_tokens_total

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Adaptive Verification  *vllm-speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index*

Speculative decoding buys fewer decode steps with more compute. At batch
size 1 that is a good trade: the GPU is memory-bound with spare compute,
so the extra draft tokens are close to free. At batch size 256 it is a
much more delicate one. Draft tokens now compete with real tokens for
the same compute, and every rejected token is compute wasted; with
enough of them, throughput drops.

That matters because per-position acceptance decays fast. While the GPU
is memory-bound that slot is effectively free and worth the gamble; once
it saturates the gamble has a real throughput cost. The crossover moves
with load and with workload-dependent acceptance rates, so no static
`num_speculative_tokens` is right across concurrencies.

Adaptive verification decides per step how much of the draft to verify
instead. Every (request, position) draft slot is scored by its survival
probability, the running product of that request's per-position
acceptance estimates, and the highest-scoring slots are admitted until a
global budget is spent. Slots compete across requests: position 5 of a
confident request can outrank position 1 of a doubtful one, so one
request keeps its full block while another could be trimmed after a
token or two.

The budget itself comes from a cost model profiled at startup. vLLM
measures what a step costs at each shape, then picks the token count
that maximizes expected accepted tokens per second.

The practical effect is that one configuration holds up across the whole
load range, which removes most of the need to tune
`num_speculative_tokens` per deployment.

SUPPORT             *speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index-support*

Adaptive verification is supported for model-based drafters (EAGLE, MTP,
DFlash, DSpark, etc.) and for
|speculative_decoding-suffix-index-adaptive-verification|. The
per-position acceptance estimates come from one of three sources:

- Confidence head: DSpark checkpoints that ship a trained confidence
  head use its predictions directly.
- Online acceptance estimator: every other model-based drafter predicts
  acceptance from its draft logits with a small logistic model,
  recalibrated during serving against the target's verification results.
- Suffix acceptance statistics: suffix decoding has no draft logits, so
  it estimates acceptance from the observed acceptance rate of past
  drafts with the same source (own history or corpus), match length and
  position.

USAGE                 *speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index-usage*

It is off by default. Enable it in the speculative config with
`enable_adaptive_verification`. For example, with DSpark:

>bash
    vllm serve deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash-DSpark \
      --tokenizer-mode deepseek_v4 --trust-remote-code \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "dspark",
        "model": "deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash-DSpark",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 7,
        "draft_sample_method": "probabilistic",
        "enable_adaptive_verification": true
      }'
<
Or with DFlash on MiMo-V2.5-Pro, whose drafter ships in the checkpoint's
`dflash/` subdirectory:

>bash
    hf download XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-V2.5-Pro-FP4-DFlash --local-dir MiMo-V2.5-Pro-FP4-DFlash

    vllm serve MiMo-V2.5-Pro-FP4-DFlash \
      --tensor-parallel-size 4 --trust-remote-code --max-model-len auto \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "dflash",
        "model": "MiMo-V2.5-Pro-FP4-DFlash/dflash",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 8,
        "enable_adaptive_verification": true
      }'
<
Set `enable_adaptive_verification: false` to verify the full block for
every request.

*speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index-requirements-and-limitations*
REQUIREMENTS AND LIMITATIONS

- The attention backend must tolerate device-decided query lengths,
  since the CPU lengths only bound them from above. Backends that plan
  off the CPU lengths are excluded by the attention selector, and
  rejected at startup for models that hard-wire their backend.
- Full cudagraphs are required: step costs are profiled from captured
  graphs, so `--enforce-eager` is rejected at startup.
- Not supported with LoRA (the per-token LoRA mapping is built from
  CPU-side boundaries) or pipeline parallelism (cost curves and
  confidences exist only on the last rank).
- Without a confidence head, `use_local_argmax_reduction` is rejected.
  The estimator needs the full draft logits, which local argmax
  reduction never materializes.

    *speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index-tuning-the-cost-profile*
TUNING THE COST PROFILE

Step costs are profiled against a synthetic KV context, 8192 tokens by
default. Deployments serving much longer contexts may want to raise it
so the profiled step reads a more realistic amount of cache (this
matters a bit less for sparse attention models like DeepSeek-v4 since
the cheap indexer is the main cost that scales with context length).

>bash
    export VLLM_ADAPTIVE_VERIFICATION_PROFILE_CONTEXT_LEN=131072
<

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
DFly                                    *vllm-speculative_decoding-dfly-index*

DFly is a checkpoint-native speculative decoder. It drafts multiple
tokens using target hidden states, sparse attention indices, and shared
KV storage. This guide covers DFly with HY4 Flash. DFly is disabled by
default; enable it with
`--speculative_config '{"num_speculative_tokens":5,"method":"dfly"}'`.
The HY4 Flash checkpoint must include DFly configuration and draft
weights.

DFly is introduced in
AngelSpec: Towards Real-World High Performance Inference with Speculative Decoding
https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25852.

USAGE                                  *speculative_decoding-dfly-index-usage*

For HY4 Flash FP8 on eight NVIDIA GPUs, with five speculative tokens:

>bash
    vllm serve tencent/Hy4-flash-FP8 \
        --tensor-parallel-size 8 \
        --dtype bfloat16 \
        --speculative_config '{"num_speculative_tokens":5,"method":"dfly"}'
<

                    *speculative_decoding-dfly-index-supported-configurations*
SUPPORTED CONFIGURATIONS

- NVIDIA GPUs and the GPU Model Runner V2.
- HY4 Flash checkpoints with compatible DFly configuration and draft
  weights.
- BF16 computation with unquantized weights, online MoE-only block FP8
  quantization, or compressed-tensors W8A8 FP8 checkpoints using 128×128
  weight blocks and dynamic group-128 activations. HY4 Flash checkpoints
  may also use weight-only MXFP4 (group-32) weights; draft layers must
  be BF16 or FP8.
- Matching target and draft KV cache dtype: BF16 (`auto` or `bfloat16`)
  or `fp8_ds_mla`. Omit the draft KV override to inherit the target
  dtype.
- Matching target and draft tensor parallelism. Pipeline, prefill
  context, and decode context parallel sizes must be one. HY4 Flash
  supports data parallelism, with each DP replica running a TP-local
  draft.
- Greedy or probabilistic draft sampling (`draft_sample_method`) with
  standard target verification. Probabilistic drafts sample at each
  request's temperature, which can improve acceptance when HY4 Flash
  samples at a nonzero temperature.

DFly currently rejects LoRA, microbatching, disaggregated
prefill/decode, heterogeneous vocabularies, adaptive verification, and a
separate draft attention backend. DFly support on ROCm has not been
validated.

Measure acceptance and latency on your own workload using the
|vllm-speculative_decoding-acceptance_metrics-index| and benchmark CLI
https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/benchmarking/cli/. This implementation
does not guarantee a speedup for every workload.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Draft Models                     *vllm-speculative_decoding-draft_model-index*

The following code configures vLLM in an offline mode to use speculative
decoding with a draft model, speculating 5 tokens at a time.

>python
    from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

    prompts = ["The future of AI is"]
    sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.8, top_p=0.95)

    llm = LLM(
        model="Qwen/Qwen3-8B",
        tensor_parallel_size=1,
        speculative_config={
            "model": "Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B",
            "num_speculative_tokens": 5,
            "method": "draft_model",
        },
    )
    outputs = llm.generate(prompts, sampling_params)

    for output in outputs:
        prompt = output.prompt
        generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
        print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}, Generated text: {generated_text!r}")
<
To perform the equivalent launch in online mode, use the following
server-side code:

>bash
    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507 \
        --host 0.0.0.0 \
        --port 8000 \
        --seed 42 \
        -tp 1 \
        --max-model-len 2048 \
        --speculative-config '{"model": "Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B", "num_speculative_tokens": 5, "method": "draft_model"}'
<
The code used to request completions as a client remains unchanged:

  Code: ~

  >python
      from openai import OpenAI

      # Modify OpenAI's API key and API base to use vLLM's API server.
      openai_api_key = "EMPTY"
      openai_api_base = "http://localhost:8000/v1"

      client = OpenAI(
          # defaults to os.environ.get("OPENAI_API_KEY")
          api_key=openai_api_key,
          base_url=openai_api_base,
      )

      models = client.models.list()
      model = models.data[0].id

      # Completion API
      stream = False
      completion = client.completions.create(
          model=model,
          prompt="The future of AI is",
          echo=False,
          n=1,
          stream=stream,
      )

      print("Completion results:")
      if stream:
          for c in completion:
              print(c)
      else:
          print(completion)
<

  Warning: ~

  Note: Please use `--speculative-config` to set all configurations
  related to speculative decoding. The previous method of specifying the
  model through `--speculative-model` and adding related parameters such
  as `--num-speculative-tokens` separately has been deprecated. For
  supported keys and examples, see the
  |features-speculative_decoding-index-speculative-config-schema|.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                *vllm-speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index*
Dynamic Speculative Decoding

*speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-why-is-dynamic-sd-needed*
WHY IS DYNAMIC SD NEEDED?

SD methods need to verify K tokens for each sequence during decoding. As
BS increases, the effective BS becomes BS*K which increases the compute
requirement during verification. When this BS*K goes beyond a critical
BS then SD negatively impacts the decode speed (TPOT). DSD helps by
tuning the K to an optimal value such that we continue to reap the
benefits from SD.

USE CASES  *speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-use-cases*

- Variable concurrency workload using same deployment. K would decrease
  as concurrency increases.
- During RL rollout where we start off with high BS but then end up with
  small BS due to very few long tail request which end up generating a
  lot of tokens stalling the progress of the current rollout. Here K
  would go up during the end of rollout.

*speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-speculative-config-schema*
`--speculative-config` SCHEMA

To use Dynamic SD, add `num_speculative_tokens_per_batch_size` to the
config of an SD method which is a list of list. Here, an entry is
`[start_bs, end_bs, optimal_K]` which means when the concurrency is
within range `[start_bs, end_bs]` then `optimal_K` number of draft
tokens are used. For e.g.,

>bash
    --speculative-config '{
        "method": "eagle",
        "model": "yuhuili/EAGLE-LLaMA3.1-Instruct-8B",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 3,
        "num_speculative_tokens_per_batch_size": [
          [1, 64, 3],
          [65, 128, 1],
          [129, 512, 0]
        ]
      }'
<
implies that:

- K=3 will be used when the concurrency is in range [1, 64]
- K=1 will be used when the concurrency is in range [65, 128]
- K=0 will be used when the concurrency is in range [129, 512], i.e., no
  draft tokens will be produced.

     *speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-online-examples*
ONLINE EXAMPLES

*speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-dynamic-sd-eagle-drafter*
Dynamic SD Eagle Drafter

>bash
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "eagle",
        "model": "yuhuili/EAGLE-LLaMA3.1-Instruct-8B",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 3,
        "num_speculative_tokens_per_batch_size": [
          [1, 64, 3],
          [65, 128, 1],
          [129, 512, 0]
        ]
      }'
<

*speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-dynamic-sd-eagle3-drafter*
Dynamic SD Eagle3 Drafter

>bash
    vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "eagle3",
        "model": "yuhuili/EAGLE3-LLaMA3.1-Instruct-8B",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 3,
        "num_speculative_tokens_per_batch_size": [
          [1, 16, 5],
          [17, 32, 4],
          [33, 64, 3],
          [65, 128, 1],
          [129, 512, 0]
        ]
      }'
<

         *speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index-limitations*
LIMITATIONS

- Tested with Eagle, Eagle-3, and DFlash. Other SD methods may or may
  not work out of the box
- Not compatible with data parallelism (`--data-parallel-size > 1`) for
  MoE models. Their DP ranks step together, but each schedules
  independently, so ranks can pick different K values, causing DP
  collective divergence and deadlocks. For MoE models with DP, vLLM
  automatically disables `num_speculative_tokens_per_batch_size` and
  falls back to the static `num_speculative_tokens` value. Dense models
  run each DP rank as an independent engine, so each rank uses its own
  schedule.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
EAGLE Draft Models                     *vllm-speculative_decoding-eagle-index*

The following code configures vLLM to use speculative decoding where
proposals are generated by an
EAGLE (Extrapolation Algorithm for Greater Language-model Efficiency)
https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.15077 based draft model. A more detailed
example for offline mode, including how to extract request level
acceptance rate, can be found in
examples/features/speculative_decoding/spec_decode_offline.py
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/main/examples/features/speculative_decoding/spec_decode_offline.py

                      *speculative_decoding-eagle-index-eagle-drafter-example*
EAGLE DRAFTER EXAMPLE

>python
    from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

    prompts = ["The future of AI is"]
    sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.8, top_p=0.95)

    llm = LLM(
        model="meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct",
        tensor_parallel_size=4,
        speculative_config={
            "model": "yuhuili/EAGLE-LLaMA3-Instruct-8B",
            "draft_tensor_parallel_size": 1,
            "num_speculative_tokens": 2,
            "method": "eagle",
        },
    )

    outputs = llm.generate(prompts, sampling_params)

    for output in outputs:
        prompt = output.prompt
        generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
        print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}, Generated text: {generated_text!r}")
<

                     *speculative_decoding-eagle-index-eagle3-drafter-example*
EAGLE3 DRAFTER EXAMPLE

>python
    from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams

    prompts = ["The future of AI is"]
    sampling_params = SamplingParams(temperature=0.8, top_p=0.95)

    llm = LLM(
        model="meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct",
        tensor_parallel_size=2,
        speculative_config={
            "model": "RedHatAI/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct-speculator.eagle3",
            "draft_tensor_parallel_size": 2,
            "num_speculative_tokens": 2,
            "method": "eagle3",
        },
    )

    outputs = llm.generate(prompts, sampling_params)

    for output in outputs:
        prompt = output.prompt
        generated_text = output.outputs[0].text
        print(f"Prompt: {prompt!r}, Generated text: {generated_text!r}")
<

PIPELINE PARALLELISM   *speculative_decoding-eagle-index-pipeline-parallelism*

EAGLE3, DFlash, and DSpark support pipeline parallelism on Model Runner
V2 with compatible target models. Auxiliary hidden states are collected
from the target model and forwarded across pipeline stages to the final
stage, where the draft model runs.

Supported targets include Llama, Qwen2, dense Qwen3, Qwen3-VL (dense and
MoE), gpt-oss, Gemma 4, and MiniMax-M3. DeepSeek-V4 and Kimi-K3 are also
supported on NVIDIA GPUs. Other targets are rejected at startup with
`<architecture> does not support <method> with pipeline parallelism`.

For example, to run gpt-oss with an EAGLE3 draft model across two
pipeline stages:

>bash
    vllm serve openai/gpt-oss-20b \
        --pipeline-parallel-size 2 \
        --speculative-config '{
            "method": "eagle3",
            "model": "RedHatAI/gpt-oss-20b-speculator.eagle3",
            "num_speculative_tokens": 3
        }'
<

             *speculative_decoding-eagle-index-pre-trained-eagle-draft-models*
PRE-TRAINED EAGLE DRAFT MODELS

A variety of EAGLE draft models are available on the Hugging Face hub:

- RedHatAI/speculator-models
  https://huggingface.co/collections/RedHatAI/speculator-models
- yuhuili/models https://huggingface.co/yuhuili/models?search=eagle

  Warning: ~

  If you are using `vllm<0.7.0`, please use this script
  https://gist.github.com/abhigoyal1997/1e7a4109ccb7704fbc67f625e86b2d6d
  to convert the speculative model and specify
  `"model": "path/to/modified/eagle/model"` in `speculative_config`.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                       *vllm-speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index*
Hidden State Extraction

The Hidden State Extraction feature allows vLLM to save intermediate
layer activations from a target model during inference. This is useful
for training |vllm-speculative_decoding-eagle-index|-style draft models,
knowledge distillation, or offline analysis of model internals.

  Note: ~

  It is possible to save the last-layer's output hidden states by
  passing `num_hidden_layers` as a layer id. Note that these are not
  normalized using the output norm.

            *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-offline-example*
OFFLINE EXAMPLE

>python
    import tempfile

    from vllm import LLM, SamplingParams
    from vllm.config.kv_transfer import KVTransferConfig
    from vllm.distributed.kv_transfer.kv_connector.v1 import (
        example_hidden_states_connector,
    )

    with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
        llm = LLM(
            model="Qwen/Qwen3-8B",
            speculative_config={
                "method": "extract_hidden_states",
                "num_speculative_tokens": 1,
                "draft_model_config": {
                    "hf_config": {
                        "eagle_aux_hidden_state_layer_ids": [1, 2, 3, 4],
                    },
                },
            },
            kv_transfer_config=KVTransferConfig(
                kv_connector="ExampleHiddenStatesConnector",
                kv_role="kv_producer",
                kv_connector_extra_config={
                    "shared_storage_path": tmpdir,
                },
            ),
        )

        outputs = llm.generate(
            ["The future of AI is"],
            SamplingParams(max_tokens=1),
        )

        for output in outputs:
            path = output.kv_transfer_params["hidden_states_path"]
            obj = example_hidden_states_connector.load_hidden_states(path)
            print(f"token_ids: {obj['token_ids'].shape}")
            print(f"hidden_states: {obj['hidden_states'].shape}")
<
A complete example is available at
`examples/features/speculative_decoding/extract_hidden_states_offline.py`
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/main/examples/features/speculative_decoding/extract_hidden_states_offline.py.

             *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-online-example*
ONLINE EXAMPLE

For improved performance, it is recommended to use a RAM-mounted file
system such as `/dev/shm/` for online usage in which the client cleans
up the files soon after they are generated.

>bash
    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3-8B \
        --speculative-config '{"method": "extract_hidden_states", "num_speculative_tokens": 1, "draft_model_config": {"hf_config": {"eagle_aux_hidden_state_layer_ids": [1, 2, 3, 4]}}}' \
        --kv-transfer-config '{"kv_connector": "ExampleHiddenStatesConnector", "kv_role": "kv_producer", "kv_connector_extra_config": {"shared_storage_path": "/dev/shm/hidden_states"}}'
<

        *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-per-request-options*
PER-REQUEST OPTIONS

Both offline and online modes support per-request options via
`kv_transfer_params`:

Parameter              Default         Description
---------------------  --------------  ---------------------------------------
hidden_states_path     Auto-generated  Custom file path for saving hidden
                                       states. If not set, files are saved to
                                       <shared_storage_path>/<request_id>.
                                       safetensors.
                                       Requires allow_custom_save_path to be
                                       enabled in the server config.
include_output_tokens  False           When True, save hidden states for both
                                       prompt and generated output tokens.
                                       When False, only prompt token hidden
                                       states are saved.

              *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-offline-usage*
Offline usage

Pass per-request options via `extra_args` on
|vllm.sampling_params.SamplingParams|:

>python
    SamplingParams(
        max_tokens=32,
        extra_args={
            "kv_transfer_params": {
                "hidden_states_path": "/tmp/my_output.safetensors",
                "include_output_tokens": True,
            }
        },
    )
<

Online usage   *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-online-usage*

Pass `kv_transfer_params` as a top-level field in the API request:

>json
    {
        "model": "Qwen/Qwen3-8B",
        "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "Hello"}],
        "max_tokens": 32,
        "kv_transfer_params": {
            "hidden_states_path": "/tmp/my_output.safetensors",
            "include_output_tokens": true
        }
    }
<

              *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-configuration*
CONFIGURATION

The `kv_connector_extra_config` dict accepts these server-level options:

Parameter                 Default  Description
------------------------  -------  -------------------------------------------
shared_storage_path       /tmp     Directory where hidden state files are
                                   saved (used when hidden_states_path is not
                                   set per-request)
allow_custom_save_path    False    Allow API clients to specify custom file
                                   paths via hidden_states_path. When
                                   disabled, client-provided paths are ignored
                                   with a warning. Enable only with trusted
                                   clients --- custom paths can write to
                                   arbitrary locations on the server.
num_writer_threads        8        Thread pool size for async disk writes
use_synchronization_lock  True     Use file locks so concurrent readers block
                                   until writes complete. Can be disabled for
                                   batch generation where synchronization is
                                   not needed.

              *speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index-output-format*
OUTPUT FORMAT

Each request produces a `.safetensors` file containing:

- `hidden_states` --- shape
  `[num_tokens, num_extracted_layers, hidden_size]`
- `token_ids` --- shape `[num_tokens]`

The file path is returned in
`output.kv_transfer_params["hidden_states_path"]`. Use
`load_hidden_states()` from the connector module to read the file with
proper synchronization.

  Note: ~

  Chunked prefill is not compatible with this feature and must be
  disabled.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
FLy Verification                         *vllm-speculative_decoding-fly-index*

FLy https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.22972 is an approximate verification
policy that can defer a native rejection at a high-entropy position when
the following draft tokens remain aligned with native verification.

  Warning: ~

  FLy is intentionally lossy. Unlike standard speculative decoding, it
  does not preserve the target distribution and may degrade model
  outputs.

USAGE                                   *speculative_decoding-fly-index-usage*

>python
    from vllm import LLM

    llm = LLM(
        model="Qwen/Qwen3-8B",
        speculative_config={
            "method": "draft_model",
            "model": "Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B",
            "num_speculative_tokens": 8,
            "rejection_sample_method": "fly",
            "fly_window_size": 6,
            "fly_entropy_threshold": 0.3,
            "fly_entropy_top_k": 3,
        },
    )
<
FLy requires at least two speculative tokens. `fly_window_size` is the
number of subsequent native acceptance decisions checked; it must be
smaller than `num_speculative_tokens` and defaults to
`min(6, num_speculative_tokens - 1)`.

The entropy gate takes the `fly_entropy_top_k` largest processed target
scores (three by default), renormalizes them into a distribution over
those candidates, and divides its entropy by `log(fly_entropy_top_k)`.
The gate value is therefore in `[0, 1]`, from 0 when the target is
certain to 1 when the top-k candidates are equally likely, and
`fly_entropy_threshold` keeps the same meaning for every
`fly_entropy_top_k`. `fly_entropy_top_k` must be positive and is capped
at the vocabulary size; with a top-k of 1 the gate value is always 0, so
FLy defers nothing unless the threshold is 0.

FLy supports greedy draft sampling with target-only acceptance and
probabilistic draft sampling with standard p/q acceptance. Token-Level
Intersection can be used for cross-vocabulary draft models with greedy
draft sampling, but is incompatible with `use_local_argmax_reduction`.

FLy has been validated with the MTP, EAGLE, DFlash, and DSpark
proposers.

 *speculative_decoding-fly-index-measuring-fly-cost-with-synthetic-acceptance*
MEASURING FLY COST WITH SYNTHETIC ACCEPTANCE

`rejection_sample_method="synthetic"` fixes the acceptance length, which
is how benchmarks pin speculative decoding to a measured acceptance
length. Setting `fly_window_size` together with `synthetic` also runs
FLy's per-step entropy computation during verification but never defers
a rejection, so the acceptance length stays exactly the synthetic one. A
benchmark pinned to FLy's measured acceptance length then also pays
FLy's verification cost:

>python
    speculative_config={
        "method": "dspark",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 8,
        "rejection_sample_method": "synthetic",
        "synthetic_acceptance_length": 4.8,
        "fly_window_size": 2,
        "fly_entropy_threshold": 0.3,
        "fly_entropy_top_k": 3,
    }
<
The same `fly_window_size < num_speculative_tokens` constraint applies.
This mode is only supported by ModelRunner V2.

                     *speculative_decoding-fly-index-validated-configurations*
VALIDATED CONFIGURATIONS

The following evaluation was run with the `draft_model` proposer of the
now-removed V1 model runner; current validation is summarized below.
Configurations not listed here are not necessarily unsupported, but have
not yet been experimentally validated.

FLy was faster than Standard SD in all 40 primary configurations. Across
all four target/draft pairs, the 29 configurations with ratio-based
quality measurements had a minimum quality retention of 97.9%.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Speculative Decoding              *vllm-features-speculative_decoding-index-2*

This document shows how to use Speculative Decoding
https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.01318 with vLLM to reduce inter-token latency
under medium-to-low QPS (queries per second), memory-bound workloads.

To train your own draft models for optimized speculative decoding, see
|vllm-speculative_decoding-speculators-index| for seamless training and
integration with vLLM.

              *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-vllm-speculation-methods*
VLLM SPECULATION METHODS

vLLM supports a variety of methods of speculative decoding. Model-based
methods such as EAGLE, MTP and draft models provide the best latency
reduction, while simpler methods such as n-gram and suffix decoding
provide modest speedups without increasing workload during peak traffic.

- |vllm-speculative_decoding-eagle-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-lilicorr-index|
- |features-speculative_decoding-index-2-dspine|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-mtp-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-dfly-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-draft_model-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-n_gram-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-suffix-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-extract_hidden_states-index|
- |features-speculative_decoding-index-2-custom-speculators-experimental|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-dynamic_speculative_decoding-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-fly-index|
- |vllm-speculative_decoding-acceptance_metrics-index|

          *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-method-selection-at-a-glance*
METHOD SELECTION AT A GLANCE

Use this qualitative table as a starting point for method selection.
Real gains depend on your model family, traffic pattern, hardware, and
sampling settings.

Method              Low QPS (latency    High QPS            Notes
                    focused)            (throughput
                                        focused)
------------------  ------------------  ------------------  ------------------
EAGLE               High gain           Medium to high      Strong
                                        gain                general-purpose
                                                            model-based
                                                            method.
MTP                 High gain           Medium to high      Best when the
                                        gain                target model has
                                                            native MTP
                                                            support.
Draft model         High gain           Medium gain         Needs a separate
                                                            draft model.
N-gram              Low to medium gain  Medium gain         Lightweight and
                                                            easy to enable.
Suffix decoding     Low to medium gain  Medium gain         No extra draft
                                                            model; matches
                                                            against prior
                                                            responses too.
Dynamic             High gain           Higher than base    Useful for RL or
Speculative                             SD method           workload with
Decoding                                                    fluctuating QPS
Adaptive            High gain           Higher than base    Sizes verification
Verification                            SD method           per request from
                                                            estimated
                                                            acceptance.

For reproducible measurements in your environment, use
`examples/features/speculative_decoding/spec_decode_offline.py`
https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/main/examples/features/speculative_decoding/spec_decode_offline.py
or the benchmark CLI guide
https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/benchmarking/cli/.

DSPINE                          *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-dspine*

DSpine drafts tokens in parallel with adjacent causal injection and
predecessor-conditioned refinement. DSpine checkpoints use the DFlash
serving path. Checkpoint metadata selects the DSpine model.

>bash
    vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B \
      --language-model-only \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "dflash",
        "model": "tencent/Qwen3.8-27B-DSpine-b16",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 15
      }'
<
Set `num_speculative_tokens` to the checkpoint's block size minus one.
Draft sampling defaults to `greedy`; set `draft_sample_method` to
`probabilistic` for probabilistic proposals. Set
`enable_adaptive_verification` to `true` in the speculative config to
enable |vllm-speculative_decoding-adaptive_verification-index|.

       *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-custom-speculators-experimental*
CUSTOM SPECULATORS (EXPERIMENTAL)

You can plug in your own speculator by registering it as a speculative
method from a |vllm-design-plugin_system-index|, and then using it with
`"method": "<name>"`.

A custom speculator consists of:

- The speculator itself: a subclass of
  `vllm.v1.worker.gpu.spec_decode.speculator.BaseSpeculator`, typically
  of `DraftModelSpeculator` (for a speculator that runs its own draft
  model, which the model runner then loads and allocates draft KV cache
  for) or
  |vllm.v1.worker.gpu.spec_decode.target_dependent_ar.speculator.TargetDependentARSpeculator|
  (for EAGLE/MTP-style drafting from the target model's hidden states).
  It's constructed with `(vllm_config, device)`.
- Its config: a subclass of
  `vllm.v1.worker.gpu.spec_decode.speculator_config.SpeculatorConfig`,
  which names the speculator class and describes how it behaves (KV
  lookahead slots, whether it needs the target's hidden states, which
  features it supports, ...). It's used in every vLLM process, including
  those that never build the speculator, so its module shouldn't import
  the speculator. A speculator that behaves like a built-in one should
  subclass that speculator's config (e.g. `Eagle3SpeculatorConfig`),
  which also determines how its draft model is configured from `model`.

>python
    # my_package/config.py
    from vllm.v1.worker.gpu.spec_decode.eagle.config import Eagle3SpeculatorConfig


    class MySpeculatorConfig(Eagle3SpeculatorConfig):
        speculator_class = "my_package.speculator.MySpeculator"
        # Accept options in `speculator_options` (validated in `apply_options`).
        supported_options = frozenset({"speculator_options"})

        @classmethod
        def apply_options(cls, spec):
            options = spec.speculator_options or {}
            if unknown := options.keys() - {"my_option"}:
                raise ValueError(f"Unknown speculator_options: {sorted(unknown)}")


    # my_package/__init__.py, registered as a `vllm.general_plugins` entry point
    def register():
        from vllm.v1.worker.gpu.spec_decode.speculator_config import (
            register_speculative_method,
        )

        register_speculative_method("my_spec", "my_package.config.MySpeculatorConfig")
<
>bash
    vllm serve <target-model> \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "my_spec",
        "model": "<draft-model>",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 3,
        "speculator_options": {"my_option": 1}
      }'
<
The speculator reads its options from
`vllm_config.speculative_config.speculator_options`. Once set up,
`speculative_config.custom_method` is the registered name (`"my_spec"`),
and `speculative_config.method` is the built-in method its config is
based on (`"eagle3"`), so vLLM treats it like that built-in speculator.

A speculator that keeps per-request state can override
|vllm.v1.worker.gpu.spec_decode.speculator.BaseSpeculator|'s no-op
request hooks, `add_request` and `on_requests_finished`, and `shutdown`.
An override of `shutdown` must call `super().shutdown()`, which releases
the draft model. A preempted request's slot is freed without a hook, and
the request is added again when it resumes, so keep such state per slot
and reset it in `add_request`.

             *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-speculative-config-schema*
`--speculative-config` SCHEMA

Use `--speculative-config` to pass speculative decoding settings as a
JSON object on the CLI:

>bash
    vllm serve <target-model> \
      --speculative-config '{
        "method": "draft_model",
        "model": "<draft-model>",
        "num_speculative_tokens": 5
      }'
<
The same keys are accepted from Python via
`LLM(..., speculative_config={...})`. The tables below highlight common
user-facing keys accepted in this JSON object; they are not an
exhaustive schema reference. For more details, see the generated
|vllm-configuration-engine_args-index| and the API docs for
|vllm.config.SpeculativeConfig|.

  Note: ~

  Gemma 4 assistant checkpoints are handled as Gemma 4 MTP speculators,
  not as generic draft models. Use `"method": "mtp"` with the assistant
  checkpoint in `model`, as shown in the

  If startup logs show `SpeculativeConfig(method='draft_model', ...)`
  for a Gemma 4 assistant checkpoint, the installed vLLM version does
  not include Gemma 4 MTP support for that path. Upgrade to a version
  that includes Gemma 4 MTP support instead of forcing the assistant
  checkpoint through generic draft-model speculative decoding.

Notes                            *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-notes*

- `--speculative-config` expects a JSON object on the CLI. In YAML
  config files, use a nested mapping instead of an escaped JSON string.
- `tensor_parallel_size` is not a valid key in `speculative_config`. Use
  `draft_tensor_parallel_size` instead.
- Keys such as `temperature` and `top_p` are sampling parameters, not
  `--speculative-config` fields.
- Internal fields such as `target_model_config`, `draft_model_config`,
  `target_parallel_config`, `draft_parallel_config`, and
  `draft_load_config` are populated by vLLM and are not intended to be
  set by users.

*features-speculative_decoding-index-2-speculative-decoding-with-pipeline-parallelism*
SPECULATIVE DECODING WITH PIPELINE PARALLELISM

With pipeline parallelism (`pipeline_parallel_size > 1`), the draft
model runs as a whole model on the last pipeline stage, as it would at
`pipeline_parallel_size=1`. A custom `VLLM_PP_LAYER_PARTITION` applies
only to the target model.

*features-speculative_decoding-index-2-lossless-guarantees-of-speculative-decoding*
LOSSLESS GUARANTEES OF SPECULATIVE DECODING

In vLLM, speculative decoding aims to enhance inference efficiency while
maintaining accuracy. This section addresses the lossless guarantees of
speculative decoding, breaking down the guarantees into three key areas:

1. Theoretical Losslessness - Speculative decoding sampling is
   theoretically lossless up to the precision limits of hardware
   numerics. Floating-point errors might cause slight variations in
   output distributions, as discussed in
   Accelerating Large Language Model Decoding with Speculative Sampling
   https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.01318

     Warning: ~

     These guarantees apply to the standard rejection sampler. FLy
     deliberately relaxes verification and is not distribution
     preserving.

2. Algorithmic Losslessness - vLLM's implementation of speculative
   decoding is algorithmically validated to be lossless. Key validation
   tests include:

     - Rejection Sampler Convergence: Ensures that samples from vLLM's
       rejection sampler align with the target distribution.
       View Test Code
       https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/47b65a550866c7ffbd076ecb74106714838ce7da/tests/samplers/test_rejection_sampler.py#L252
     - Greedy Sampling Equality: Confirms that greedy sampling with
       speculative decoding matches greedy sampling without it. This
       verifies that vLLM's speculative decoding framework, when
       integrated with the vLLM forward pass and the vLLM rejection
       sampler, provides a lossless guarantee. Almost all of the tests
       in tests/spec_decode/e2e
       https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/tree/main/tests/v1/spec_decode
       verify this property using this assertion implementation
       https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/b67ae00cdbbe1a58ffc8ff170f0c8d79044a684a/tests/spec_decode/e2e/conftest.py#L291

3. vLLM Logprob Stability - vLLM does not currently guarantee stable
   token log probabilities (logprobs). This can result in different
   outputs for the same request across runs. For more details, see the
   FAQ section titled Can the output of a prompt vary across runs in
   vLLM? in the |vllm-usage-faq-index|.

While vLLM strives to ensure losslessness in speculative decoding,
variations in generated outputs with and without speculative decoding
can occur due to following factors:

- Floating-Point Precision: Differences in hardware numerical precision
  may lead to slight discrepancies in the output distribution.
- Batch Size and Numerical Stability: Changes in batch size may cause
  variations in logprobs and output probabilities, potentially due to
  non-deterministic behavior in batched operations or numerical
  instability.

For mitigation strategies, please refer to the FAQ entry Can the output
of a prompt vary across runs in vLLM? in the |vllm-usage-faq-index|.

         *features-speculative_decoding-index-2-known-feature-incompatibility*
KNOWN FEATURE INCOMPATIBILITY

1. Pipeline parallelism is not composable with speculative decoding as
   of `vllm<=0.15.0`
2. Speculative decoding with draft models is not supported in
   `vllm<=0.10.0`

- A Hacker's Guide to Speculative Decoding in vLLM

- What is Lookahead Scheduling in vLLM?
  https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Z9TvqzzBPnh5WHcRwjvK2UEeFeq5zMZb5mFE8jR0HCs/edit#heading=h.1fjfb0donq5a
- Information on batch expansion
  https://docs.google.com/document/d/1T-JaS2T1NRfdP51qzqpyakoCXxSXTtORppiwaj5asxA/edit#heading=h.kk7dq05lc6q8
